# Parity audit: Monaco & CodeMirror 5

Generated from a 9-domain, 18-agent three-way comparison of this editor against local checkouts of
[CodeMirror 5](https://github.com/codemirror/codemirror5) and [Monaco/VS Code](https://github.com/microsoft/vscode)
(`references/codemirror5`, `references/vscode` — gitignored).

Every claimed gap was re-checked by a second agent against this codebase to filter false gaps;
13 statuses were corrected and ~20 findings dropped as false or not worth pursuing.

**Status legend** — `missing`: no equivalent here. `partial`: we have machinery that covers some of it.
**Effort** is scaled to *our* architecture, not the reference's.

| Findings | High value | Missing | Partial |
|---|---|---|---|
| 101 | 58 | 47 | 52 |

## Text model, buffer & undo

### Huge-file guards decided once at construction and permanently respected

`partial` · value **high** · effort **S** · performance · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Three independent budgets, all resolved once in the constructor from the buffer's length and line count so no later edit can flip the answer mid-session: tokenization is refused above 20MB *or* 300k lines; worker syncing is refused above 50MB; and whole-document string materialization (`getValue`, `getLinesContent`) throws above 256M characters rather than OOMing the renderer. The line-count threshold matters as much as the byte threshold — a 5MB minified bundle is one line and tokenizes fine, a 5MB log file with 400k lines does not.

**Why it matters here.** Nothing in our editor asks how big the document is before doing something proportional to its size. Concretely: `findController` calls `host.materializeFullText()` on every search input change, and after any edit that string is not retained, so each call walks the piece tree and joins a fresh full-size string. The diff plugin, decode plugin, merge-conflict plugin and the shiki worker client all do the same. On a 50MB file, opening find allocates 50MB per keystroke. There is also no point at which we decline to tokenize or decline to ship the document to a worker.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/textModel.ts:188-191 (_MODEL_SYNC_LIMIT 50MB, LARGE_FILE_SIZE_THRESHOLD 20MB, LARGE_FILE_LINE_COUNT_THRESHOLD 300k lines, LARGE_FILE_HEAP_OPERATION_THRESHOLD 256M chars), :334-348 (the decision, with the comment '!!! Make a decision in the ctor and permanently respect this decision !!!'), :368 (_isTooLargeForSyncing), :633-643 (the three predicates), :651 (tokenization refuses), :801-803 and :871-873 (getValue / getLinesContent throw BugIndicatingError when isTooLargeForHeapOperation)

**Our code.** grep for `1024 * 1024|MAX_FILE|tooLarge|LARGE_|maxFileSize|sizeLimit` across packages/*/src returns nothing. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/find/src/findController.ts:375 and :449 call `host.materializeFullText()` unconditionally; /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/mergeConflictPlugin.ts:156,195; /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/diff/src/editorDiffPlugin.ts:156; /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/decode/src/index.ts:73; /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/shiki/workerClient.ts:262,327-329. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/documentTextSnapshot.ts:42 only retains the string for the *initial* snapshot (`materializedText?.length === snapshot.length`), so every post-edit call re-materializes.

**Verifier.** Downgrade from missing to partial and re-scope. The syntax budget Monaco wants is already ours and better — do not add a size cutoff there, it would replace graceful windowing with a cliff. What to keep is the third budget only: a materialization guard. The strong signal that we already know this is a problem is documentTextSnapshot.ts:44-52, which instruments every full-text read via `recordFullTextSnapshotRead`/`measureEditorPerformance` with a hit/miss flag — we measure the hazard but never refuse it. Cheapest correct shape for us is not Monaco's throw-above-256M but making `materializeFullText()` size-gated and pushing find/diff/merge-conflict onto the chunk API that already exists on the same object (`forEachTextChunk`, documentTextSnapshot.ts:15) — find in particular is a streaming scan that never needed the string.

**How to implement.** Compute `{ length, lineCount }` once in `createEditorTextBuffer` (documentSession.ts:258-269) — line count is already `getSubtreeLineBreaks(root) + 1`, free from the tree — and expose `isTooLargeForTokenization()`, `isTooLargeForSyncing()`, `isTooLargeForHeapOperation()` on `EditorTextBuffer` / the plugin host context (packages/editor/src/plugins.ts:275 already declares `materializeFullText` on the host interface, so add the predicates beside it). Gate `refreshSyntax` (Editor.ts) and the worker sync paths on them. For find specifically, the bigger win is not a guard but a chunked search: `TextSnapshot.forEachTextChunk` already exists and streams piece text without joining — port Monaco's line-by-line search shape (textModelSearch.ts:229-253) on top of it, keeping full materialization only for multiline regex sources (`isMultilineRegexSource`, textModelSearch.ts:69).

### EOL detection, normalization, and BOM stripping at buffer-construction time

`missing` · value **high** · effort **M** · correctness · from **monaco**

**What it is.** The builder counts cr, lf and crlf occurrences while ingesting chunks. `_getEOL` picks '\r\n' if `cr + crlf > totalEOL/2`, else '\n'. If the chosen EOL disagrees with what's in the text, every chunk is rewritten with `str.replace(/\r\n|\r|\n/g, eol)` and its lineStarts array rebuilt, so the tree is internally single-EOL. The BOM is stripped off the first chunk and stored separately on the buffer (`getBOM()`), then re-prepended only by `createSnapshot(preserveBOM)` / `getValue(eol, preserveBOM)`. `acceptChunk` refuses to end a chunk on a lone '\r' or a high surrogate, holding the char back so no chunk boundary ever falls inside a CRLF pair or a surrogate pair. When normalization is off, `_EOLNormalized === false` and every line read strips a trailing CR with an explicit regex. `setEOL` re-runs `normalizeEOL`, and the EOL is recorded in the undo stack (beforeEOL/afterEOL in editStack.ts:41-47) so undo restores it.

**Why it matters here.** Open any file written on Windows or any git checkout with `core.autocrlf=true` and our editor silently keeps a '\r' as the last character of every line. Because our view computes line ends as `nextLineStart - 1`, that CR is part of the rendered line text: the caret can be placed after it, End goes one column too far, word/subword navigation sees a spurious character, find/replace regexes with `$` misbehave, and the character is round-tripped back on save. There is also no BOM handling, so a UTF-8 BOM becomes a visible zero-width character at offset 0 of line 1 that all offsets are shifted by.

Worth flagging that the damage is bigger than a cosmetic one: because /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextViewModel.ts computes lineEndOffset as nextLineStart-1, a CR sits INSIDE the rendered line, so on a CRLF file every End key, every selection-to-line-end, every column measurement, and every gutter width is off by one, and the stray CR reaches the DOM. ### Undo coalescing keyed on edit-operation type, so consecutive deletes merge and an explicit undo-stop API exists

`partial` · value **high** · effort **M** · feature · from **both**

**What it is.** Monaco keeps one *open* edit-stack element and appends every subsequent edit into it (`compressConsecutiveTextChanges`, editStack.ts:50-56) until something closes it. The cursor decides: before each operation it computes `shouldPushStackElementBetween(previousOpType, newOpType)`. The rules are (a) always stop before a non-typing operation, (b) `TypingFirstSpace` never forces a stop — so a single space glues the preceding word to the following one — while a *second* space does, and (c) otherwise stop whenever the normalized operation type changes. Deletes have their own types: consecutive Backspaces are all `DeletingLeft` so they collapse into one undo step, consecutive Deletes are all `DeletingRight`, and switching between them, or deleting a non-empty selection, forces a stop. Any command can also call `pushStackElement()` to force a boundary. CM5 reaches a similar place differently: everything inside one `operation` (same `opId`) merges unconditionally, and across operations it merges by a caller-supplied `origin` string protocol — `'+input'`/`'+delete'` merge if within `historyEventDelay` ms, `'*'`-prefixed always merge, anything else never merges. That origin string is the public extension point for undo grouping.

**Why it matters here.** Today every Backspace is its own undo step in our editor. Hold Backspace over a word, then press Cmd-Z: Monaco and CM5 restore the whole word in one stroke; we restore one character at a time. The same applies to Delete, to indent/outdent runs, and to any repeated programmatic edit. Our typing coalescing also has no time component, so a run typed 10 minutes apart at the same offset still merges into one undo entry. And plugins have no way to say 'these three edits are one user action' — `undoGroup` is declared in the metadata type but is never written by any caller.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/cursor/cursorTypeEditOperations.ts:942-976 (getTypingOperation / shouldPushStackElementBetween / normalizeOperationType); cursorCommon.ts:34-41 (EditOperationType: Other, DeletingLeft, DeletingRight, TypingOther, TypingFirstSpace, TypingConsecutiveSpace); cursorDeleteOperations.ts:22 and :172 (`shouldPushStackElementBefore = prevEditOperationType !== EditOperationType.DeletingLeft/Right`, forced true at :35/:184 when the selection was non-empty); editStack.ts:412-420 (_getOrCreateEditStackElement appends into the open element), :390-408 (pushStackElement/popStackElement close and reopen it); references/codemirror5/src/model/history.js:61-99 (addChangeToHistory: merge if same opId, or same `origin` where '+'-prefixed origins merge within historyEventDelay and '*'-prefixed always merge) and src/edit/options.js:155 (historyEventDelay 1250)

**Our code.** /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/documentSession.ts:552-565 `shouldAmendTypingRun` returns false unless `options.metadata.intent === 'insert-text'`, so backspace/delete/indent/outdent transactions (intents at :208-217) always push a fresh history entry via :544-549; :567-584 `updateTypingRun` nulls the run for any non-insert intent; :217 `readonly undoGroup?: string` — grep across packages shows the only other reference is Editor.ts:2482 reading it, never writing it; :1178-1182 `startsNewWordAfterWhitespace` is our only boundary heuristic and it puts the stop *after* whitespace rather than before it.

**Verifier.** Keep, but narrow the scope to the half that is actually missing. Do NOT build an undo-stop API — we have one. The real defect is that holding Backspace over a word produces one undo entry per character, so Cmd-Z restores one character at a time; Monaco merges the whole deletion run. The fix is small and local: generalize `shouldAmendTypingRun`/`updateTypingRun` (documentSession.ts:552-583) from a single 'insert-text' intent to a typing-run *kind* ('insert' | 'backspace' | 'delete'), amend only when kind matches and the edit is contiguous with the run (for backspace that means `edit.to === typingRun.startOffset` rather than `edit.from === endOffset`), and keep the existing `startsNewWordAfterWhitespace` boundary. Monaco's `TypingFirstSpace` nuance — a single space does not force a stop but a second one does — is worth copying since our current heuristic puts the stop after whitespace rather than before it.

**How to implement.** Generalise `TypingRun` (documentSession.ts:243-246) into `{ opType: 'typing' | 'typing-space' | 'delete-left' | 'delete-right' | 'other', endOffset, startOffset }` and replace `shouldAmendTypingRun` with a `shouldContinueRun(prevOpType, nextOpType)` port of Monaco's table. Backspace/delete already flow through `commitRecordedEdit`, so amending them is the same `amendEditorHistory` call already used for typing — the only new piece is a delete-aware `createAmendedTypingTransaction` that extends the recorded range leftward (for backspace) or rightward (for delete) and re-derives `inverseEdits` by concatenating the removed text. Add a public `breakUndoGroup()` on DocumentSession (we already have `breakTypingRun`) and start honouring `metadata.undoGroup` as CM5 honours `origin`: same non-empty group => always amend, different group => always break. Add a wall-clock guard (`Date.now() - runStartedAt > 1250`) to match historyEventDelay.

### Bounded undo history — depth cap, and serializing closed stack elements out of the live heap

`missing` · value **high** · effort **M** · performance · from **both**

**What it is.** CM5 hard-caps the number of undo events at 200 and shifts the oldest off, carefully removing the selection entry that pairs with it. Monaco does not cap count but converts each undo element to a compact binary blob as soon as it stops being the append target (`close()` — which is exactly the moment the undo group ends), so the *live* representation of history is a byte buffer rather than a graph of JS objects, and `heapSize()` gives the service a real number to budget against.

**Why it matters here.** Our undo stack is an unbounded persistent linked list where each entry retains a whole `PieceTableSnapshot` **and** a `DocumentTransaction` containing `snapshotBefore`, `snapshotAfter`, `edits` and `inverseEdits` (the inverse edits hold the full deleted text as strings). Structural sharing makes the tree cheap, but two things are not shared: the inverse-edit strings, and — more importantly — deleted pieces are never removed from the tree. `deleteFromPieceTable` marks pieces invisible (tombstones) instead of unlinking them, so `pieceCount` only ever grows, and every retained historical root pins every tombstone that was ever created. A long editing session on a large file therefore grows monotonically in both node count (slowing every O(log n) descent) and string retention, with no eviction point anywhere. The tombstone-compaction TODO is already acknowledged in the code but has nothing to trigger it.

**Reference.** references/codemirror5/src/model/history.js:16 (undoDepth), :90-94 (`while (hist.done.length > hist.undoDepth) { hist.done.shift(); if (!hist.done[0].ranges) hist.done.shift() }` — pops the change *and* its paired selection entry) and src/edit/options.js:154 (`option("undoDepth", 200, ...)`); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/editStack.ts:82-140 (SingleModelEditStackData.serialize/deserialize into a flat ArrayBuffer with fixed-width selections and length-prefixed text), :200-210 (close() serializes when the element stops being appendable, open() rehydrates), :235-240 (heapSize() = byteLength + 168 overhead); vs/editor/common/services/modelService.ts:502-542 (sums element.heapSize() and drops the retained stack when it exceeds maxMemory)

**Our code.** /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/history.ts:30-53 `pushHistoryEntry`/`commitEditorHistory` push unconditionally; `size` is tracked but never compared to a limit and nothing ever truncates `undo`. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/textbuffer/src/edits.ts:112-141 `deleteFromPieceTable` calls `markTreeInvisible` (tree.ts:402-418) which keeps every node. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/textbuffer/src/walker.ts:8-9 comments reference an unimplemented 'tombstone-compaction TODO'. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/documentSession.ts:220-228 DocumentTransaction retains both snapshots plus edits and inverseEdits.

**Verifier.** Confirmed, but lower the priority and drop Monaco's serialization half. Monaco binary-serializes closed elements because its undo elements own full text strings; ours do not — text lives in the append-only piece buffers regardless of history, and snapshots share tree nodes structurally, so a retained entry costs a handful of objects, not a copy of the document. The genuine cost is second-order: because history pins every historical snapshot, no tombstone can ever be compacted, so the walker's O(k) boundary cost (walker.ts:8-9) grows without bound in a long editing session — a day-long session on a hot file degrades sequential reads. So the item to keep is a depth cap that lets the tail of history be dropped and thereby unblocks the tombstone-compaction TODO, not blob serialization. CM5's cap is 200 events; note it also has to evict the paired selection entry, which we would not, since our entries carry selections inline (history.ts:400-403).

**How to implement.** Add `maxUndoDepth` (default ~200) to `createEditorHistory` and truncate in `pushHistoryEntry` — since the stack is a persistent cons list, truncation means walking to the tail once and rebuilding, so instead track depth and rebuild only every N pushes, or switch `EditorHistoryStack` to a plain array with `shift()` like CM5. Truncation is also the natural trigger for tombstone compaction: once the oldest retained root is dropped, run a rebuild pass (flattenPieces filtered on `visible`, then `createTreeFromPieces` + `buildReverseIndex`) on the *current* snapshot only, and invalidate any anchors resolving to now-dropped pieces via the existing `liveness: 'deleted'` path in anchors.ts:113-135. Separately, stop retaining `snapshotBefore`/`snapshotAfter` on transactions that are already in the history stack — the stack holds them.

### Surrogate-pair-aware range validation before an edit is applied

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **S** · correctness · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Before any edit or decoration range reaches the buffer it is snapped so that neither endpoint falls between the high and low halves of a surrogate pair. The rules are asymmetric on purpose: a non-empty range *expands* to swallow the whole code point (so deleting 'half an emoji' deletes the emoji), while an empty range (a cursor) *moves left* rather than expanding, because expanding a cursor would turn it into a selection.

**Why it matters here.** We validate code-point boundaries only when creating an anchor, and we do it by throwing. That means a caret can be created mid-pair only via a crash, but an *edit* whose range splits a surrogate pair is accepted silently and produces a lone surrogate in the buffer — permanently corrupt text that then breaks every downstream `slice`, the tree-sitter worker's UTF-8 conversion, and LSP position mapping. Reachable paths: an LSP server returning a UTF-16 offset computed against a different encoding, a regex replace whose match boundary lands mid-pair, and our own commands doing offset arithmetic (`offset - 1` for backspace) near an emoji or a CJK extension character.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/textModel.ts:175-184 (StringOffsetValidationType.Relaxed vs SurrogatePairs), :1028-1062 (_validatePosition moves a position off the low half of a surrogate pair), :1119-1160 (_validateRange: if the start is mid-pair expand left, if the end is mid-pair expand right, if both expand both, and if the range is *collapsed* mid-pair move it left instead of expanding it), :1064-1078 (validateRange/validatePosition always use SurrogatePairs), :925-984 (_validateRangeRelaxedNoAllocations, the fast path used for decorations)

**Our code.** /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/textbuffer/src/anchors.ts:33-45 `ensureCodePointBoundary` throws `RangeError('anchor offset must be a code-point boundary')` — it is called only from `anchorAt` (:200), never from the edit path. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/textbuffer/src/edits.ts:31-43 `validateBatchEdits` checks only ordering and `ensureValidRange` (reads.ts:11-15 = bounds only). /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/documentSession.ts:1234-1238 `normalizeTextEdits` only sorts.

**Verifier.** Keep, but as cheap defence-in-depth rather than a user-visible bug fix. Our own cursor and word-motion code cannot produce a mid-surrogate offset — that is well covered and tested (packages/editor/test/editor-helpers.test.ts:250,664; packages/editor/test/textRanges.test.ts:19). The exposure is entirely *external* offsets: LSP text edits converted through packages/lsp/src/positions.ts (UTF-16 code-unit positions from a server that may split a pair), find-and-replace ranges, and plugin-supplied edits. Today those either silently corrupt the buffer into a lone surrogate or, if they happen to route through anchorAt, throw a RangeError at an unhelpful place. Adopt Monaco's asymmetry precisely — non-empty range expands outward to swallow the code point, empty range moves left — and put it in `validateBatchEdits` (edits.ts:31-43) so every entry point inherits it. Small, contained, no architectural impact.

**How to implement.** Add `snapEditRangeToCodePointBoundaries(snapshot, from, to)` next to `ensureValidRange` in packages/textbuffer/src/reads.ts, reusing the two-char probe already written in anchors.ts:33-45. Apply Monaco's exact rules: if `from === to` and mid-pair, move both to `from - 1`; otherwise expand `from` left and `to` right as needed. Call it from `validateBatchEdits` (edits.ts:31) so every path — session applyEdits, insert, delete — is covered, and change `ensureCodePointBoundary` in `anchorAt` from throwing to snapping so a stray offset degrades instead of crashing the editor.

### Model-level indentation guessing (tabSize + insertSpaces) with an alignment-vs-indentation heuristic

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **S** · feature · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Rather than reading a config value, the model derives indentation from the content: for each pair of consecutive non-blank lines it computes the indentation delta, discards deltas that look like column alignment rather than nesting, scores each candidate tab size by how often that delta appears, and picks the highest-scoring one with a tie-break preferring even sizes. The tabs-vs-spaces decision is a separate majority vote over lines whose indentation contains a tab vs lines with >1 space. The 10k-line sample cap keeps it O(1)-ish on huge files, and the cons-string note is a real V8 detail: `charCodeAt` on a rope forces flattening, so for lines over 64KB it reads through the buffer's own accessor.

**Why it matters here.** Our indentation unit is whatever `tabSize` the host passes in, so opening a 2-space file in a 4-space-configured editor silently indents new lines wrong and our indent/outdent commands operate on the wrong unit. This is the single most visible 'the editor doesn't understand my file' behaviour, and it is self-contained — no architecture change, one function over the text.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/indentationGuesser.ts:105-215 (guessIndentation: samples at most 10k lines, ALLOWED_TAB_SIZE_GUESSES = [2,4,6,8,3,5,7] preferring even sizes, a spacesDiffCount score array indexed by candidate tab size, tab-vs-space vote from linesIndentedWithTabsCount vs linesIndentedWithSpacesCount), :17-90 (spacesDiff: compares consecutive non-blank lines' indentation, bails when a line mixes tabs and spaces, divides spacesDiff by tabsDiff when both changed, and flags `looksLikeAlignment` when the previous line ends in a comma and the extra spaces line up under a token — so `const a = b,\n      d = c;` does not vote for tabSize 6), :123-125 (`useCurrentLineText = currentLineLength <= 65536` — for very long lines it reads char codes off the buffer instead of forcing V8 to flatten a cons-string)

**Our code.** /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/editor/indentation.ts:67-71 `indentUnit(base, tabSize)` returns `'\t'` or `' '.repeat(tabSize)` from the passed option; :29 `readonly tabSize: number` is an input. grep for `detectIndent|guessIndent` across packages returns nothing.

**Verifier.** Upgrade to partial: we already infer tabs-vs-spaces, just locally. That local rule is arguably better than Monaco's document-wide vote for the common case (it does the right thing in a mixed file), so the gap is narrower than claimed and the fix is smaller: derive tabSize from content at load, and keep indentUnit's local rule as-is. Two concrete payoffs beyond indentUnit. First, outdent and scope-guide levels currently use a possibly-wrong configured tabSize (documentSelectionEdits.ts:261-262 reads exactly `tabSize` characters of prefix; scope-lines/src/index.ts:384-385 floors column/tabSize) — on a 2-space file with tabSize 4 the indent guides land on the wrong columns. Second, lsp-plugin/src/formatting.ts:48 sends `insertSpaces: true` unconditionally to every language server, so formatting a tab-indented file silently converts it. Monaco's alignment-vs-indentation discard step is the non-obvious part worth copying verbatim; without it, aligned function arguments poison the histogram.

**How to implement.** Port `guessIndentation` almost verbatim against our `TextSnapshot` — it needs only `getLineCount`, `getLineLength` and `getLineContent`, all of which the virtualization model already exposes (virtualizedTextViewModel.ts:47-49 `lineText` / `lineStartOffset` / `lineEndOffset`), and the view already holds the `lineStarts` array so line access is O(1). Run it once when a document is loaded (Editor.setText, Editor.ts:667) and feed the result into the existing `IndentationOptions` as a default that an explicit host setting overrides. Keep the 10k-line sample cap and the `looksLikeAlignment` rule — without the latter the guesser mis-fires badly on aligned declaration lists and on markdown bullet lists.

### Position<->offset conversion caches: a node search cache, a last-visited-line cache, and a remainder trick that avoids the second tree descent

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **M** · performance · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Three layers. (1) The augmented tree stores `lf_left` (line feeds in the left subtree) and `size_left`, so both directions are a single root-to-node walk. (2) `getPositionAt` gets the column for free from the in-piece remainder returned by `getIndexOf`, and only falls back to a second `getOffsetAt` descent in the boundary case where the offset sits on the piece's first line. (3) A tiny LRU (limit 1) of `{node, nodeStartOffset, nodeStartLineNumber}` short-circuits repeated lookups in the same neighbourhood — which is what actually happens during typing, multi-cursor edits, and viewport rendering — and `validate(offset)` invalidates only entries at or past the edit rather than clearing everything.

**Why it matters here.** Our `offsetToPoint` does two independent full descents: `countLineBreaksBeforeOffset` and then `lineStartOffset` (which is a third helper doing its own descent to find the row-th line break). `pointToOffset` does two descents (line start + line end). These run per selection per line in indent/outdent (documentSelectionEdits.ts:237-250), and per fold and per edit in foldMap/inlineMap. With 500 cursors, an indent is ~2000 tree descents, all in the same handful of pieces. There is no cache anywhere at this layer — the good `lineStarts` index we do maintain lives in the *view* (virtualization), so model-side consumers like the fold map and selection edits get none of it.

### Buffer-level `mightContainRTL` / `mightContainNonBasicASCII` flags that unlock a per-line rendering fast path

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **M** · performance · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Three monotone booleans maintained on the buffer, computed for free during the line-start scan at load and updated only from newly inserted text (never re-derived from the whole document). They are cheap negatives: `mightContainRTL === false` is a proof, so the view can skip bidi handling entirely; `isBasicASCII` per line lets the renderer skip grapheme/width analysis. The layering matters — the expensive `containsRTL` regex is only run on chunks already known to be non-ASCII. Separately, U+2028/U+2029 are detected and the model offers to strip them (they are line terminators to the JS engine but not to the editor, a classic source of 'the file looks fine but breaks').

**Why it matters here.** We do no ASCII/RTL/unusual-terminator classification at all, so every line goes down the same rendering and measurement path. For the overwhelmingly common case — a source file that is pure ASCII — a proof of that fact is the cheapest possible optimization for text measurement, caret geometry and the minimap, and it costs nothing to compute because we already scan the whole document for '\n' at load. U+2028/U+2029 also currently pass straight through: they are not line breaks to us but they are to `String.prototype.split` and to any JS the content might become.

The prerequisite is BiDi geometry itself, which is a large independent project and the actual finding hiding behind this claim. (3) The one genuinely cheap, genuinely broken item is unusual line terminators: U+2028/U+2029 are line breaks to the browser's layout engine but not to our model, so a document containing one renders with a visual line our model does not know exists, desynchronizing row geometry from row indices — the same class of bug as the CRLF issue in claim 1, and fixable in the same load-time scan (virtualizedTextViewHelpers.ts:233-243 `computeLineStarts`). Then thread `mightContainNonBasicASCII` into the row renderer (packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextViewRows.ts) as the gate for any per-grapheme work, and into the minimap renderer. Note our `computeLineStartsFromSnapshot` currently scans char-by-char with `text[index] !== '\n'`, which allocates a one-character string per character; switching it to `indexOf('\n')` (as the sibling `computeLineStarts` already does) is an independent, free win on large files.

### Chunked change buffer with re-chunking, and typed-array line-start indexes

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **M** · performance · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Two ideas. Chunk sizing: 64KB is chosen because `substring`/`charCodeAt` degrade on very large strings and because V8 cons-strings from repeated appends must be flattened before indexed access; splits deliberately never land inside a CRLF or a surrogate pair. Re-chunking: whenever the buffer is normalized, the whole tree is walked and re-emitted into evenly sized chunks in the `[min, 2*min]` band, which collapses the fragmentation that accumulates from thousands of small inserts. Index representation: line-start arrays are `Uint32Array` (or `Uint16Array` when the chunk is small), roughly half the memory of a V8 `number[]` and with better cache behaviour on the binary searches that run per position lookup.

**Why it matters here.** Our initial document is one single unchunked string (`PieceBufferChunkStore.from([original])`), and our per-buffer line index is a plain `number[]` covering the *whole* file. For a 300k-line file that is a 300k-element JS array (~2.4MB as doubles, vs 1.2MB as Uint32Array) built eagerly on the first line-break query, and the same shape is duplicated in the view's `lineStarts`. Our insert path does chunk at 16KB and coalesces into the tail chunk, which is good, but there is no re-chunking pass ever, so a long session leaves a tree of thousands of small pieces that never consolidate — compounding the tombstone problem, since neither pieces nor tombstones are ever merged back together.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/pieceTreeTextBuffer/pieceTreeBase.ts:14 (AverageBufferSize = 65535), :1164-1200 (createNewPieces splits any inserted text over 64KB into chunks, and refuses to split on a trailing \r or high surrogate), :855-868 (the append fast path requires `value.length < AverageBufferSize` and that the insert continues exactly where the last change ended, `_lastChangeBufferPos`), :320-353 (normalizeEOL re-chunks the entire tree into chunks between `min = size - size/3` and `max = 2*min`, i.e. it *rebalances* rather than preserving the historical chunking), :37-62 (createLineStartsFast returns a Uint32Array/Uint16Array via createUintArray, not a number[]); references/codemirror5/src/model/chunk.js:110-120 (splices are avoided on huge leaf arrays — 'small slices are taken... in order because sequential memory accesses are fastest')

**Our code.** /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/textbuffer/src/buffers.ts:11 `BUFFER_CHUNK_SIZE = 16 * 1024` (used only for inserts, :244-277), :320-332 `createInitialBuffers` does `PieceBufferChunkStore.from([original])` — one chunk for the entire file; :52-55 `PieceBufferLineIndex = { offsets: number[]; scannedLength: number }`; :81-110 in edits.ts `tryCoalesceInsert` correctly caps the tail at BUFFER_CHUNK_SIZE and refuses to coalesce into the original buffer. No re-chunking or piece-merging pass exists anywhere in packages/textbuffer/src/.

**Verifier.** Correctly partial, but split it: three sub-items with very different value. (a) Re-chunking the original buffer: low value for us, because the lazy per-buffer line index (buffers.ts:141-174) already removes the main reason Monaco re-chunks, and V8 sliced strings make substring on a large original cheap. Skip. (b) Split-point safety: genuinely wrong today — buffers.ts:251 can split a surrogate pair across two chunks on a large paste, and while the walker reassembles code points across piece boundaries (walker.ts, tested), anything reading a single chunk directly sees a lone surrogate. Cheap one-line guard, worth doing. (c) Typed-array line indexes: real memory win at scale (offsets as number[] on a 5M-line file is ~40MB of boxed doubles vs 20MB as Uint32Array), and we already have the pattern in packedTokens.ts to copy, so the cost is low. Do (b) and (c), skip (a).

**How to implement.** Three independent pieces. (1) Change `PieceBufferLineIndex.offsets` to a growable Uint32Array (with a documented fallback to number[] above 4GB, which we will never hit) — the only consumers are the binary search helpers in buffers.ts:177-215, so the change is local. (2) Chunk the original text in `createInitialBuffers` at the same 16KB (or raise to 64KB to match Monaco's reasoning about substring cost) and split on '\n' boundaries where possible so per-chunk line indexes stay small; `createOriginalPiece` becomes a loop like `appendChunksToBuffers`. (3) Add a `compactPieceTable(snapshot)` that walks visible pieces in order, merges adjacent pieces that are contiguous in the same buffer, drops tombstones, and re-emits evenly sized chunks — then call it from the undo-truncation point proposed in the bounded-history finding, so compaction has a safe trigger where no historical snapshot can still reference the dropped pieces.

### Decorations as an interval tree with lazy subtree deltas and a four-way stickiness taxonomy

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **L** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Decorations live in an augmented red-black tree keyed by absolute offset, but each node stores its position *relative* to the accumulated `delta` of its ancestors. An edit therefore does not touch every decoration after it: it adds one delta to O(log n) subtree roots. `maxEnd` augmentation makes 'all decorations overlapping [a,b)' an O(log n + k) query, which is what the renderer needs per viewport. Per-decoration stickiness is a real taxonomy — AlwaysGrowsWhenTypingAtEdges, NeverGrowsWhenTypingAtEdges, GrowsOnlyWhenTypingBefore, GrowsOnlyWhenTypingAfter — decomposed into two independent booleans (does the start stick to the previous char, does the end), plus `forceMoveMarkers` for 'push this decoration even if it would normally stay' and `collapseOnReplaceEdit` for 'if an edit swallows me whole, become empty at the edit start'. The `commonLength` phase handles replacements correctly: for `replace(3 chars, 5 chars)` the first 3 chars are treated as overwritten in place, so a decoration inside them doesn't get flung to the end.

**Why it matters here.** Our range decorations are plain `{start, end}` arrays that plugins recompute and hand over wholesale — there is no persistent decoration store, so nothing survives an edit on its own, every plugin re-derives its ranges after every change, and the view has no way to ask 'what decorations touch rows 400-450' other than scanning the whole array. Our anchors are the right primitive for tracking (they already survive edits and report liveness), but they expose only `bias: 'left' | 'right'` — one bit where Monaco has two plus two override flags — so a plugin cannot express 'this squiggly should grow when I type inside it but not when I type at its edges', which is exactly the difference between a diagnostic that stays sensible while you edit and one that doesn't.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/intervalTree.ts:154-177 (node.delta — every node's start/end is relative to its parent chain), :56-64 (the comment on why deltas must be normalized: they grow/shrink until 30-bit overflow), :306-332 (acceptReplace: pull out the nodes intersecting the edit, shift everything else by a single delta on O(log n) subtree roots via noOverlapReplace, then re-insert the touched nodes), :339-345 and :350-380 (_normalizeDeltaIfNecessary / normalizeDelta), :396-411 (adjustMarkerBeforeColumn with MarkerDefined/ForceMove/ForceStay), :416-489 (nodeAcceptEdit: the full start/end stickiness resolution including collapse-on-replace and the commonLength phase for replacements), :492-520 (searchForEditing using maxEnd to prune)

**Our code.** /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/editor/types.ts:37-42 `EditorRangeDecoration` is `{className?, start, end, style?}` — plain offsets, no identity, no stickiness. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/editor/rangeDecorations.ts is 126 lines of grouping/equality helpers over arrays, no index. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/editor/src/editor/Editor.ts:2101-2110 `composedRangeDecorations` concatenates every projection's array on each read. Tracking primitive exists: /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/textbuffer/src/pieceTableTypes.ts:12 `AnchorBias = 'left' | 'right'` and anchors.ts:113-135 resolve with liveness.

**Verifier.** Correctly partial, but reframe the work as *wiring*, not invention. We do not need to build an interval tree or design a stickiness taxonomy — a per-endpoint AnchorBias of left|right already spans exactly Monaco's four TrackedRangeStickiness values (verified at references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model.ts:676-677), since start-bias x end-bias = the same four growth behaviours. The missing piece is that `EditorRangeDecoration` is a value object with no identity, so a plugin cannot say 'this decoration, follow the text' — every plugin re-derives offsets after each edit, which is why composedRangeDecorations rebuilds by concatenation. The high-value change is giving decorations an id plus start/end anchors, and reusing the tokenIndex maxEnds machinery (tokenIndex.ts + packedTokens.ts:59-74) for the viewport query rather than writing a new red-black tree. That is a contained refactor with a clear payoff for find-highlights, LSP diagnostics, document-highlights and inline replacements, all of which currently churn full arrays.

**How to implement.** Don't port the interval tree wholesale — our anchors already solve the tracking half, and a persistent treap keyed on resolved offset would fight the immutable-snapshot design. Two targeted steps instead. (1) Widen `AnchorBias` to Monaco's four-way stickiness by making it a pair of biases (startSticksToPrevious, endSticksToPrevious) at the *range* level rather than the anchor level, and port `nodeAcceptEdit`'s commonLength phase into a `TextRange.applyEdit` helper in packages/editor/src/textRanges.ts (257 lines, already the home for range math) so plugins that keep plain offsets can shift them correctly without re-deriving. (2) Give the view an interval index over the composed decoration array — since it is rebuilt per frame anyway, a sorted array plus a running `maxEnd` prefix gives the same O(log n + k) viewport query for a few dozen lines of code, replacing the current full scan in virtualizedTextViewHighlights.ts.
