## Decorations, widgets & zones

### ZoneWidget: view zone reserves whitespace, the DOM lives in a stable overlay widget driven by onDomNodeTop — our block-row DOM is destroyed on scroll recycle

`partial` · value **high** · effort **M** · correctness · from **monaco**

**Removed subsystem.** The block-surface subsystem (`editorBlocks.ts`, `blockSurfaceController.ts`, `virtualizedTextViewBlockLanes.ts`, `setBlockRows`, `EditorBlockHorizontalSurface`) was deleted from this repo. Paths below that named it are gone; the finding is kept for the Monaco/CM comparison it records.

**What it is.** Monaco never puts widget DOM inside the scrolled line list. A view zone contributes only *whitespace* to the vertical layout; its own DOM node is a stub. The real content is registered as an overlay widget (viewport-fixed, absolutely positioned), and the zone's `onDomNodeTop(top)` callback pushes the scrolled top onto that overlay node each render. Consequences: the widget's DOM is created once and never re-parented, so focus, text selection, an embedded editor's scroll position, IME composition and iframe state all survive scrolling; the widget can be clipped to the editor bounds; and resizing via a sash only changes the whitespace height, not the row list. `ZoneWidget` then layers on the reusable chrome — arrow (a dynamically generated `.arrow-decoration-N` CSS rule positioned via a decoration), frame borders, `_decoratingElementsHeight()` subtracting arrow+frame from the body height, `_getMaximumHeightInLines()` capping at 80% of the viewport, and `revealRange` after showing. `PeekViewWidget` subclasses it into title bar + action bar + body with `_doLayoutHead/_doLayoutBody`.

**Why it matters here.** The deleted block surfaces mounted into recycled virtual rows, so any interactive surface (a diff comment box, an inline chat input, an embedded preview, a peek result list) lost focus and internal state the moment the user scrolled it out and back. That ceiling is the main reason the subsystem is gone. The lesson carries to whatever replaces it: reserve space in the row list, but host the DOM in a stable overlay so it is never re-parented.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/zoneWidget/browser/zoneWidget.ts:385-460 (_showImpl: creates an *empty* viewZoneDomNode, registers it as a view zone, then registers `this.domNode` as an OverlayWidgetDelegate), :288-300 (_onViewZoneTop sets domNode.style.top, _onViewZoneHeight re-lays out the body); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/viewParts/viewZones/viewZones.ts:357-410 (render: every zone node stays in the DOM forever, only top/height/display are updated, plus onDomNodeTop(-1000000) when off-screen); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/codelens/browser/codelensWidget.ts:190-260, :291-296 (same split: CodeLensViewZone reserves px, CodeLensContentWidget renders ABOVE line n+1); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/peekView/browser/peekView.ts:98-272 (PeekViewWidget adds _fillHead/_fillBody/_doLayoutHead/_doLayoutBody/setTitle/onDidClose on top of ZoneWidget)

**Our code.** Nothing. We have no space-reserving zones at all since the block-surface deletion, and no overlay-hosted variant or ZoneWidget/PeekView abstraction anywhere in the repo (grep for `zoneWidget`/`peek` in `packages/*/src` returns nothing). The virtualizer retains the variable-row-height machinery (`rowSizes`, `rowHeightIndex.ts`) that a replacement primitive would reserve space through.

**Verifier.** Was the highest-value item in this domain, and the recycling defect it names is exactly what the deletion cleared. It stands as the design constraint on the replacement: a surface holding component state (an editable form, a scrolled diff pane, a video, a focused input) must not live in a recycled row. Monaco's full ZoneWidget is not required — the row list reserves the height, and the surface DOM lives in a viewport-fixed overlay that the row render pushes `top` onto, mirroring onDomNodeTop.

**How to implement.** Design the replacement overlay-first rather than retrofitting: the widget's DOM is mounted once into a viewport-fixed layer (the content-widget layer from finding #3, or a sibling) and kept alive for the widget's lifetime, while the row list contributes only a spacer of the measured height via `rowSizes`. Wire the per-frame top from the virtualizer's `item.start`, and set `display:none` plus `onTop(-1e6)` when the widget leaves the viewport (Monaco's exact trick, viewZones.ts:392-397). `ZoneWidget` is then ~150 lines on top: an abstract class with `showArrow`/`showFrame` options, `_fillContainer`, `_doLayout(height,width)`, and a `PeekViewWidget` subclass adding the title/action bar.

### Edit-tracked decoration identity: interval tree + 4-way stickiness + collapseOnReplaceEdit

`partial` · value **high** · effort **L** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** A decoration is a persistent object with an id, an ownerId, and options. Ranges live in a red-black interval tree keyed by offset, where each node stores `start`/`end` relative to its parent via a `delta` field so a text edit only rewrites O(log n) deltas rather than every node (`noOverlapReplace`), plus `maxEnd` for interval search pruning. `acceptReplace(offset,length,textLength,forceMoveMarkers)` removes only the nodes intersecting the edit, shifts everything else by delta arithmetic, then re-inserts the intersecting nodes after running `nodeAcceptEdit` on each. `nodeAcceptEdit` implements four stickiness modes (AlwaysGrows / NeverGrows / GrowsOnlyWhenTypingBefore / GrowsOnlyWhenTypingAfter) by deriving `startStickToPreviousCharacter` and `endStickToPreviousCharacter` and applying `adjustMarkerBeforeColumn` three times (once at edit start, once at the common prefix of delete/insert, once at edit end) with MarkerMoveSemantics of ForceMove/ForceStay/MarkerDefined. Extra semantics: `collapseOnReplaceEdit` makes a decoration collapse to a point when an edit fully encompasses it (intervalTree.ts:437-444), and `forceMoveMarkers` pushes markers past an insertion at their exact offset. Stickiness/isForValidation/isInGlyphMargin/affectsFont/collapseOnReplaceEdit are all bit-packed into a single `metadata` number to keep nodes cache-friendly. Callers never touch the tree: they use `changeDecorations(accessor => accessor.deltaDecorations(old, new))` or an `IEditorDecorationsCollection`, which diffs and reuses ids.

**Why it matters here.** Today every consumer in our repo re-derives its ranges from scratch after each edit, in its own code, with its own bugs. `packages/lsp-plugin/src/diagnosticProjection.ts` hand-projects diagnostic ranges through `DocumentSessionChange.edits`; find, occurrences, document links, merge conflicts and minimap decorations each keep raw offsets and rely on their owner re-emitting a whole array. A shared tracked-range service would delete all of that duplicated projection logic, make decorations survive edits during the async gap before an LSP response arrives (so squiggles stop flickering/mis-aligning while typing), and give every future feature (bookmarks, inline chat anchors, code lens) correct behaviour for free.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/intervalTree.ts:305-331 (IntervalTree.acceptReplace), :416-470 (nodeAcceptEdit), :121-138 (stickiness packed into node.metadata bitfield), :141-235 (IntervalNode with delta/maxEnd/cachedAbsoluteStart), :345-380 (normalizeDelta); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model.ts:675-680 (TrackedRangeStickiness enum), :417-444 (IModelDecorationsChangeAccessor), :1081-1100 (changeDecorations/deltaDecorations/removeAllDecorationsWithOwnerId); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/editorCommon.ts:544-578 (IEditorDecorationsCollection); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/textModel.ts:2197-2236 (DecorationsTrees)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/editor/types.ts:37-42` — `EditorRangeDecoration` is `{start, end, className?, style?}`: plain numbers, no id, no stickiness, no owner. `packages/editor/src/editor/Editor.ts:496` sets them wholesale from `options.rangeDecorations`; `Editor.ts:2103-2107` (`composedRangeDecorations`) just concatenates every projection's array each frame. We DO have piece-identity anchors with left/right bias and deletion liveness (`packages/textbuffer/src/anchors.ts:1-261`, `anchorBefore`/`anchorAfter`/`resolveAnchor`), but a repo-wide grep shows only two consumers: `packages/editor/src/inlineMap.ts:1,191,230-231` and `packages/editor/src/foldMap.ts:1`. Nothing else tracks ranges across edits.

**Verifier.** Real gap is narrower than claimed: no SHARED store, no per-decoration id/owner, no interval tree with `delta`/`maxEnd` (every projection is O(n) per edit and re-supplied wholesale by its owner), and stickiness is hard-coded per feature rather than a 4-way selector. The two existing projections prove the semantics are already understood here, so this is consolidation, not invention. Note our `displayProjectionRegistry` already carries owner/layer/priority — one level too coarse, as the claim correctly says.

**How to implement.** Don't port the red-black interval tree — we already have a stronger primitive. Build `packages/editor/src/editor/trackedRanges.ts` on top of `pieceTable/anchors`: a `TrackedRange = {id, owner, start: Anchor, end: Anchor, options}` store keyed by id, with `deltaRanges(oldIds, newSpecs)` mirroring Monaco's accessor. Map the four stickiness modes onto anchor bias pairs (AlwaysGrows = anchorBefore(start)/anchorAfter(end); NeverGrows = anchorAfter(start)/anchorBefore(end) — which is exactly what `inlineMap.ts:191` already hardcodes; the two asymmetric modes are the mixed pairs), and add `collapseOnReplaceEdit` + `forceMoveMarkers` on top since anchors alone don't express them. `resolveAnchor` already returns `liveness: 'deleted'`, which is the natural signal for dropping a decoration. Then register the store as a `rangeDecorations` display projection (`displayProjectionRegistry.ts`) so the existing render path is unchanged, and port `diagnosticProjection.ts` and `occurrenceHighlights.ts` to it as the first two consumers.

### One decoration object, many surfaces — text, glyph margin, line margin, minimap, overview ruler, injected text

`partial` · value **high** · effort **L** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** There is exactly one decoration API. The options object fans out to every rendering surface, and the model pre-partitions storage by the expensive query: `_decorationsTree0` for normal decorations, `_decorationsTree1` for overview-ruler decorations, `_injectedTextDecorationsTree` for anything with `before`/`after` — so the view-model's per-line injected-text query never walks the diagnostics. Cheap boolean flags for rarer surfaces are bit-packed into the node metadata (`IsForValidationMask`, `IsMarginMask`, `AffectsFontMask` in intervalTree.ts:280-320) so `getAllMarginDecorations` is a filtered tree walk rather than a full scan. The change event carries `affectsMinimap` / `affectsOverviewRuler` / `affectsGlyphMargin` / `affectedInjectedTextLines`, letting each view part skip re-render when a decoration change cannot possibly touch it, and `beginDeferredEmit`/`endDeferredEmit` coalesce a whole batch of decoration mutations into one event.

**Why it matters here.** A plugin author who wants 'error on line 12' currently has to talk to three unrelated registries in our repo and keep them in sync by hand — and there is no way at all to also mark it in the scrollbar. Unifying means a single `setDecorations` call lights up squiggle + gutter icon + minimap tick + (future) overview ruler, and the three surfaces can never drift out of sync during edits.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model.ts:149-310 (IModelDecorationOptions: className, inlineClassName, beforeContentClassName/afterContentClassName, before/after InjectedTextOptions, glyphMarginClassName + glyphMargin lane, linesDecorationsClassName, lineNumberClassName, marginClassName, minimap, overviewRuler, zIndex, isWholeLine, showIfCollapsed, lineHeight, fontFamily/fontSize/fontWeight, blockClassName/blockPadding/blockIsAfterEnd, textDirection); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model/textModel.ts:2197-2273 (DecorationsTrees splits into three trees + filters by affectsFont/lineHeight/showIfCollapsed); :2586-2641 (DidChangeDecorationsEmitter records _affectsMinimap/_affectsOverviewRuler/_affectsGlyphMargin/_affectedInjectedTextLines and defers)

**Our code.** We have the fan-out concept but as N disjoint registries, each with its own type and its own source/owner keying: `packages/editor/src/editor/displayProjectionRegistry.ts:6-13` enumerates five independent projection kinds (`rangeDecorations`, `rowDecorations`, `injectedRows`, `gutters`, `folds`) with separate payload types; the minimap keeps a *fourth* registry entirely (`packages/minimap/src/plugin.ts:297-306` `decorationsBySource`, `EditorMinimapDecoration` from `packages/minimap/src/types.ts:60`). `VirtualizedTextRowDecoration` (`packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextViewTypes.ts:100-103`) is just `{className?, gutterClassName?}` — no zIndex, no minimap, no lane. Positively, `displayProjectionRegistry.ts:57-64` already carries `owner`, `layer`, `priority`, and `invalidationRange` per projection, which is the ownerId/zIndex idea one level too coarse (per-array instead of per-decoration). No overview ruler exists anywhere (grep for `overviewRuler` across `packages/` returns nothing).

**Verifier.** Keep, but scope to the storage-partitioning insight (separate trees so a per-line injected-text query never walks diagnostics), not a unification rewrite. Our per-projection owner/layer/priority is genuinely good design; the missing piece is per-decoration granularity.

**How to implement.** Widen `EditorRangeDecoration` (`packages/editor/src/editor/types.ts:37`) into an `EditorDecoration` carrying optional `glyphMargin`, `lineDecoration`, `minimap`, `overviewRuler`, `zIndex`, `isWholeLine`, `showIfCollapsed` alongside the existing className/style, and make the tracked-range store from finding #1 the single source. Then have the existing consumers *derive* from it rather than be fed separately: `Editor.applyRangeDecorations` (Editor.ts:2065-2083) filters to the text-styling subset, the gutter contribution path filters on `glyphMargin`, and `MinimapDecorationRegistry` becomes a subscriber instead of a parallel store. Keep three separate indexes internally exactly as Monaco does so the hot per-row injected-text lookup does not scan diagnostics, and add `affects*` flags to whatever change signal we emit so `virtualizedTextView` can skip work.

### Content widgets: an editor-managed layer for position-anchored floating UI with declarative fit preferences

`missing` · value **high** · effort **L** · feature · from **monaco**

**What it is.** A widget declares only `getPosition() → {position, secondaryPosition?, preference: [ABOVE, BELOW, EXACT]}`; the editor owns all geometry. Each render it computes the anchor's top/left/lineHeight from the *view* position (so folding and word-wrap are handled), measures the widget once (or takes `beforeRender()`'s answer, avoiding a forced reflow), then runs `_layoutBoxInViewport` or `_layoutBoxInPage` to get `{fitsAbove, aboveTop, fitsBelow, belowTop, left}` with the left clamped into the scroll viewport. The placement loop runs **twice** over the preference list: pass 1 accepts a preference only if it actually fits, pass 2 accepts the first preference regardless — so 'prefer below, fall back to above, else force below' is expressed as data, not branching. `_reduceAnchorCoordinates` handles a *secondary* anchor (e.g. suggest widget wants to touch both the cursor and the word start) by nudging left toward the secondary while guaranteeing the primary still touches. `allowEditorOverflow` + `fixedOverflowWidgets` moves the node into a separate overflow container using `position: fixed` with page coordinates, so a suggest list can escape a small editor. `afterRender(position, coordinate)` tells the widget which side it actually landed on, so it can flip its own arrow/shadow.

**Why it matters here.** Every floating UI we ship re-implements this badly and independently: hover tooltips, completion list, signature help, rename input, find widget. They compute their own DOMRect, clamp their own viewport, and each must remember to re-anchor on scroll/layout. Centralizing gets us consistent flip-when-it-doesn't-fit behaviour, correct behaviour when the anchor line is folded or scrolled out, and one place to fix shadow-DOM/overflow-clipping bugs — plus it makes third-party plugin widgets a two-line affair.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/editorBrowser.ts:126-176 (ContentWidgetPositionPreference EXACT/ABOVE/BELOW, IContentWidgetPosition with primary+secondary position), :177-232 (IContentWidget: allowEditorOverflow, useDisplayNone, suppressMouseDown, beforeRender→IDimension, afterRender(position, coordinate)); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/viewParts/contentWidgets/contentWidgets.ts:308-333 (_layoutBoxInViewport), :356-400 (_layoutBoxInPage with 15px lateral / 22px vertical clearance and fixedOverflowWidgets), :403-440 (_getAnchorsCoordinates), :442-456 (_reduceAnchorCoordinates), :458-520 (_prepareRenderWidget two-pass fit loop)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/plugins.ts:210-236` — `EditorViewContributionContext` gives a plugin `container`, `scrollElement`, and `getRangeClientRect(start,end): DOMRect | null` and nothing else; there is no managed widget layer. Each consumer rolls its own: `packages/lsp-plugin/src/hoverDefinitionController.ts:274`, `packages/lsp-plugin/src/completionController.ts:204`, `packages/lsp-plugin/src/signatureHelpController.ts:186`, `packages/lsp-plugin/src/plugin.ts:488` all call `getRangeClientRect` and then position by hand. `packages/lsp-plugin/src/tooltip.ts:38-41` hardcodes its own `TOOLTIP_GAP_PX`, `TOOLTIP_VIEWPORT_MARGIN_PX`, `TOOLTIP_MAX_HEIGHT_PX` and a `preferredPlacement: 'top' | 'bottom'` — a private one-off of Monaco's preference list. Each controller separately re-anchors on `'viewport' | 'layout'` update kinds (`completionController.ts:78`).

**Verifier.** Value is LOWER than the claim implies. tooltip.ts already uses CSS anchor positioning — `anchor-name` (:259), `position-anchor` (:561), `position-area` (:650) — so the browser performs Monaco's `_layoutBoxInViewport` for free. What is actually duplicated across four controllers is only the flip-on-overflow decision and the max-height clamp (tooltip.ts:569-652). Build a ~100-line shared `anchoredSurface` helper, not Monaco's widget layer. Skip Monaco's `beforeRender()` forced-reflow avoidance — anchor positioning sidesteps it.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/src/editor/contentWidgets.ts` owning a single `position: absolute` layer inside `scrollElement` (plus a `position: fixed` overflow layer appended to `document.body` for `allowEditorOverflow`). Expose `addContentWidget/removeContentWidget/layoutContentWidget` on `EditorViewContributionContext`, and drive re-layout from the existing `EditorViewContributionUpdateKind` fan-out in `viewContributions.ts:82-95` — 'viewport', 'layout', 'content' and 'selection' all already fire there, which is exactly Monaco's onScrollChanged/onLinesChanged set. Reuse `inputSelection.rangeClientRect` (`Editor.ts:1979`) for the anchor rect and the virtualizer's row geometry for `lineHeight`. Port `_layoutBoxInViewport` and the two-pass preference loop verbatim — they are ~60 lines and fully self-contained. Then delete the bespoke positioning from `tooltip.ts`, `completionController.ts`, `signatureHelpController.ts` and `renameWidget.ts`.

### Injected text as a first-class concept: phantom content at a zero-width anchor, with cursor stops and hit-test payload

`partial` · value **high** · effort **L** · feature · from **monaco**

**What it is.** `before`/`after` on a decoration inject text into the *view* only — the model text, offsets and LSP positions are untouched. Crucially the anchor range may be **empty** (`showIfCollapsed: true`), which is what makes inlay hints possible at all: the hint hangs off a point, not a span. `cursorStops` controls whether the caret can land on either side of the injected run, so arrowing through `foo(⟨name:⟩ 1)` skips the phantom text as one unit. `attachedData` is an arbitrary object recoverable from `getInjectedTextDecorations`/the decoration under the mouse, which is how clicking an inlay hint part resolves back to its `InlayHintLabelPart` for go-to-definition — the hit-testing story that any interactive phantom text needs. `inlineClassNameAffectsLetterSpacing` tells the layout engine the class changes advance widths so measurement is redone. The same primitive powers color swatches, ghost text, inline progress placeholders, and whitespace padding (an injected U+200A with a width-only CSS class).

**Why it matters here.** Inlay hints (parameter names, inferred types) are the single most-requested modern editor feature and we structurally cannot render them today. The same primitive would also give us CSS colour swatches, inline error text at end-of-line, and ghost text for AI completions — all currently impossible without faking them as whole extra rows.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model.ts:316-360 (InjectedTextOptions: content, inlineClassName, inlineClassNameAffectsLetterSpacing, attachedData, cursorStops; InjectedTextCursorStops Both/Right/Left/None); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/inlayHints/browser/inlayHintsController.ts:536-560 (addInjectedText builds a decoration with showIfCollapsed + collapseOnReplaceEdit + AlwaysGrowsWhenTypingAtEdges and `[item.anchor.direction]: opts`), :561-566 (addInjectedWhitespace injects U+200A with a width-only class), :599-706 (per-part padding/border-radius, maximumLength trimming with '…', fixed-length column alignment), :411-418 (_getInlayHintLabelPart recovers `attachedData instanceof RenderedInlayHintLabelPart` from the decoration under the mouse); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/colorPicker/browser/colorDetector.ts:203-243 (color swatch = `before: {content: noBreakWhitespace, inlineClassName: dynamicRule}`); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/inlineProgress/browser/inlineProgress.ts:23-31 + :91-98 (injected nbsp reserves the space, a content widget at EXACT draws the spinner in it)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/inlineMap.ts:186-190` — `inlineRangeFromSpec` does `if (endOffset <= startOffset) return null`, so an `InlineReplacementSpec` must cover at least one real character. Our inline layer *substitutes* existing text (markdown `**` → hidden, `- ` → `•`, see `packages/markdown/src/replacements.ts:103-122`), it cannot *insert* phantom text. `InlineReplacement` (`packages/editor/src/displayTransforms.ts:173-180`) carries `{id, startColumn, endColumn, text, kind?, metadata?}` — no `className`, so injected content cannot be styled differently from surrounding code, and no cursor-stop control (only a global `TransformBias` at `inlineMap.ts:131-142`). `InjectedTextRow` (`displayTransforms.ts:97-106`) injects a whole extra *row* before/after a line, not inline content at a column. `metadata` exists on both, which is the `attachedData` idea already half-present.

**Verifier.** Correct framing: the substrate (atomic segments, total column mapping in both directions, group reveal, metadata passthrough, kind→CSS) is all built; one guard and a zero-width segment kind stand between us and inlay hints. Styling caveat the claim missed in the other direction: replacement text renders into the row's plain text chunks, NOT its own span, so per-span styling (an inlay-hint chip with its own background/padding) needs a new chunk kind. `cursorStops` per side is genuinely absent — we only have the global `TransformBias` at inlineMap.ts:131-142.

**How to implement.** Relax `inlineRangeFromSpec` to accept `endOffset === startOffset` when the spec carries an explicit `insert: true` (or a `showIfCollapsed` flag), anchoring both ends at the same offset with opposing biases so typing at the point pushes the hint aside rather than through it. Add `className` and `cursorStops: 'both' | 'left' | 'right' | 'none'` to `InlineReplacementSpec`/`InlineReplacement`, and thread `className` into the segment rendering in `createInlineRow` (`displayTransforms.ts:318-340`) so injected runs get their own span class. `cursorStops` then becomes an override on the `TransformBias` used by `inlineColumnToSourceColumn` for caret motion — our existing `revealInlineMap` machinery must NOT reveal insertion-kind specs, so gate reveal on `kind`. `metadata` already gives us `attachedData`; expose an `inlineReplacementAtPoint(clientX, clientY)` lookup for hit-testing. With that, an inlay-hints controller in `packages/lsp-plugin` is a straightforward LSP `textDocument/inlayHint` consumer emitting specs through the existing display-projection registry.

### Preserve the visual anchor when content height changes above the viewport

`missing` · value **medium** · effort **S** · correctness · from **codemirror5**

**What it is.** Whenever a line widget is added, removed or resized, CodeMirror checks whether the widget's line sits *above* the current scrollTop. If so it adds the height delta straight into scrollTop, so the pixels the user is looking at do not move. `LineWidget.clear()` applies the negative delta and `LineWidget.changed()` applies the delta on resize — all three mutation paths are covered, and the compensation happens inside the same operation as the height change, so there is no intermediate frame where the content has jumped.

**Why it matters here.** This is exactly the jank users notice: a fold collapses 200 lines above the cursor, or a row resolves to a taller measured height — and the text under the user's eyes jumps. It is a small, self-contained fix with a large perceived-quality payoff, and it becomes more important the moment variable-height rows have a producer again.

**Reference.** references/codemirror5/src/model/line_widget.js:53-57 (adjustScrollWhenAboveVisible: if heightAtLine(line) < scrollTop, addToScrollTop(cm, diff)), :59-76 (addLineWidget computes aboveVisible before updateLineHeight and compensates by the widget height), :36-49 (LineWidget.changed() recomputes height and applies the same compensation for a resize)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/virtualization/fixedRowVirtualizer.ts:796-807` — `nextScrollTopForOptions` only clamps: `if (options.scrollMode === 'static') return 0; return clampScrollTopForGeometry(currentScrollTop, scrollGeometryForOptions(options, viewportHeight))`. When `setOptions` receives new `rowSizes` (which is how non-uniform row heights enter the layout via `createRowHeightIndex`), the scrollTop is carried over unchanged, so any height delta above the viewport shifts everything down. Folding above the viewport goes through the same path, and so will any future variable-height row producer. The virtualizer does track a `stableVirtualWindow` and does scroll bookkeeping (`fixedRowVirtualizer.ts:240-290`) but purely for emit-throttling, not for anchoring.

**Verifier.** Best value-to-effort ratio in this domain. The defect is live today for folding a region above the viewport, independent of any lazily-measured row. CM5's fix is 4 lines: if the changed row's top is above scrollTop, add the height delta to scrollTop. Our `rowHeightIndex` already gives O(log n) offset-of-row, so we can compute the delta exactly rather than CM5's per-widget bookkeeping.

**How to implement.** In `fixedRowVirtualizer.setOptions` (around line 168), before adopting the new normalized options, compute the old and new `rowHeightIndexStart(index, firstVisibleRow)` for the row that was at the top of the viewport (or better: for the row containing the primary caret when it is visible, else the top row) and add the difference to `scrollTop` before clamping. `rowHeightIndex.ts` already exposes `rowHeightIndexStart(index, row)` and `rowHeightIndexRowAtOffset(index, offset)`, so this is a handful of lines. Guard it behind 'the anchor row's start moved but the anchor row index is unchanged' so a genuine document replacement still resets to the top. Add a test in `virtualizedTextViewModel.test.ts` asserting that collapsing a fold above the viewport leaves the visible first row unchanged.

### Range-highlight paint order is registration-order dependent, so overlapping decorations can swap z-order after scrolling

`missing` · value **medium** · effort **S** · correctness · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Monaco makes stacking an explicit, declared property of each decoration and sorts by it deterministically at render time, then merges only same-zIndex peers. Order never depends on when a decoration was created or whether it was momentarily empty.

**Why it matters here.** We paint range decorations through the CSS Custom Highlight API, where paint order for equal-priority highlights is *registration order in the `CSS.highlights` registry*. Our code never sets `Highlight.priority`, and it deregisters a group whenever it momentarily has zero mounted ranges — then re-registers it later, which moves it to the end of the registry. Concrete repro: with a find-match highlight overlapping the current selection, scroll all matches out of the mounted window (group hits `highlight.size === 0` → `unregisterRangeHighlight`), then scroll back; the group re-registers *after* the selection highlight and now paints on top of it, inverting the colours the user saw a moment ago. The same applies between any two overlapping groups — diagnostics vs occurrences, bracket match vs selection.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/model.ts:200-206 (zIndex: 'A decoration with greater stack order is always in front of a decoration with a lower stack order when the decorations are on the same line'); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/viewParts/glyphMargin/glyphMargin.ts:24-37 (DecorationToRender carries zIndex), :299-323 (requests sorted by lineNumber, laneIndex, zIndex DESC, type DESC, className — with an explicit 'don't change this sort unless you understand prepareRender' warning), :356-375 (only decorations sharing the winner's zIndex are merged)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextViewHighlights.ts:1306-1315` — `ensureRangeHighlightRegistered` does `view.highlightRegistry.set(group.name, group.highlight)` with no `group.highlight.priority` assignment; grep for `priority` in `packages/editor/src/virtualization/*.ts` returns nothing. `renderRangeHighlight` (:260-279) calls `unregisterRangeHighlight` whenever `group.highlight.size === 0` — i.e. whenever no range of that group is currently mounted — and re-registers on return. Group names are minted as `${prefix}-range-${semanticName}-${index}` where `index` is `groups.length` at build time (`packages/editor/src/editor/rangeDecorations.ts:69-79`), and `groupedRangeDecorations` (:12-31) only coalesces *adjacent* decorations sharing a style key, so a differently-ordered input array produces differently-numbered groups. `EditorRangeDecoration` (`packages/editor/src/editor/types.ts:37-42`) has no zIndex field; the only ordering signal we have is the per-projection `layer`/`priority` in `displayProjectionRegistry.ts:62-63`, which never reaches the highlight registry.

**Verifier.** Cheapest real bug in the list: assign `group.highlight.priority` from the owning projection's layer/priority in `ensureRangeHighlightRegistered`. Note our substrate differs from Monaco's — we do not merge same-zIndex peers into one span, we stack ::highlight() pseudo-elements, so we need priority propagation, not Monaco's sort-and-merge.

**How to implement.** Two-part fix. (1) Add `zIndex?: number` to `EditorRangeDecoration`, default it from the owning projection's `layer`/`priority` in `Editor.composedRangeDecorations` (`Editor.ts:2103-2107`), include it in `rangeDecorationGroupKey` (`rangeDecorations.ts:81-91`), and set `group.highlight.priority = zIndex` in `ensureRangeHighlightRegistered`. That makes paint order declared rather than incidental. (2) Stop deregistering on transient emptiness — in `renderRangeHighlight` (:272-276) keep the group registered with an empty `Highlight` when it has ranges in the model but none currently mounted, and only deregister in `clearRangeHighlight`. Sort groups by zIndex in `groupedRangeDecorations` before assigning indices so group names are stable across frames too. A regression test can assert `CSS.highlights` iteration order/priority is unchanged across a mount-out/mount-in cycle.

### Refcounted dynamic CSS rule factory: arbitrary per-decoration styling without unbounded stylesheet growth

`present` · value **medium** · effort **S** · performance · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Features that need genuinely dynamic styling (a colour swatch whose background is the literal parsed colour; an inlay hint whose font-size/padding/border-radius derives from options) cannot use static CSS classes. `DynamicCssRules` hashes the property bag, reuses an existing rule for identical properties, hands out `{className, dispose}` and garbage-collects only after a 1s delay — which is the key trick: a re-render that disposes and immediately re-creates the same rule hits the cache instead of churning the stylesheet. `ThemeColor` values become CSS custom-property references rather than literals, so a theme switch needs no rule regeneration at all.

**Why it matters here.** Our decoration styling is limited to three properties applied through `::highlight()`, which cannot express borders, padding, border-radius, inline-block widths, or `::before` content — everything an inlay hint, colour swatch, or ghost-text run needs. A refcounted rule factory is the missing enabler for findings #5 and #6, and it prevents the obvious naive implementation (inline styles per span, or a stylesheet that grows monotonically) from becoming a memory leak on a long-lived editor.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/editorDom.ts:284-345 (DynamicCssRules: _rules keyed by JSON.stringify(properties), createClassNameRef increments a refcount and returns a disposable, a RunOnceScheduler garbage-collects unreferenced rules after 1000ms so hanging rules can be reused), :347-400 (CssProperties whitelist; RefCountedCssRule creates one <style> element per rule and resolves ThemeColor values to `var(--vscode-…)` CSS variables, and targets the shadow root when the editor is in shadow DOM); usage: inlayHintsController.ts:571-706 (a class ref per hint part), colorDetector.ts:200-243 (a class ref per colour swatch)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/editor/rangeDecorations.ts:44-56` — `rangeDecorationStyle` extracts exactly `backgroundColor`, `color`, `textDecoration`; `VirtualizedTextHighlightStyle` (`packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextViewTypes.ts:94-98`) has the same three fields. We do dedupe by style key within a frame (`rangeDecorationGroupKey`, :81-91) and rebuild the sheet via `rebuildStyleRules(view)` (`virtualizedTextViewHighlights.ts:257,288,368,450,472`), so there is a per-frame dedupe but no cross-frame refcounted cache and no delayed GC — every style change rewrites the whole rule set. The lsp-plugin instead copies a fixed list of theme CSS variables onto its tooltip element by hand (`packages/lsp-plugin/src/tooltip.ts:44-54`), which is the ad-hoc version of Monaco's ThemeColor→CSS-variable indirection.

**Verifier.** Reframe entirely: the factory exists and is good; the work is routing range decorations through it and widening the property set. Also flag the real ceiling the claim mis-diagnosed — our styling substrate is `::highlight()`, and the CSS Highlight pseudo-element only honours a small property set (no border-radius, padding, or box model). So Monaco's motivating cases (an inlay-hint chip, a colour swatch) need a per-span element regardless of how rules are cached; that is claim 5/6 territory, not this one.

**How to implement.** Port `DynamicCssRules` almost verbatim into `packages/editor/src/style-utils.ts` (which already exists for style helpers) — it is ~110 lines with no VS Code dependencies beyond `DisposableStore` and a debounced scheduler, both trivial to replace. Key by `JSON.stringify` of a sorted property bag, keep the 1s delayed GC, and resolve theme tokens to the `--editor-*` custom properties we already define (`tooltip.ts:44-54` lists them). First consumers: the injected-text `className` from finding #5, and a widened `EditorRangeDecoration.style` for anything the Highlight API cannot paint. Note this only helps for *span-rendered* decorations — `::highlight()` pseudo-elements accept a narrow property set by spec — so it lands together with an inline-span rendering path, not as a replacement for the highlight path.

### View zone knobs we lack: ordinal tiebreak, before-first-line, render-in-hidden-areas, margin twin, min scroll width, suppressMouseDown

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **M** · api-design · from **monaco**

**Removed subsystem.** The block-surface subsystem (`editorBlocks.ts`, `blockSurfaceController.ts`, `virtualizedTextViewBlockLanes.ts`, `setBlockRows`, `EditorBlockHorizontalSurface`) was deleted from this repo. Paths below that named it are gone; the finding is kept for the Monaco/CM comparison it records.

**What it is.** Zones are inserted into the layout's whitespace list, not the line list, so they compose with folding and wrapping. `afterLineNumber: 0` places a zone above line 1 — the only way to render a header. `ordinal` (defaulting to `afterColumn`, else 10000) deterministically orders multiple zones anchored to the same line, which matters the moment two independent features both want a zone there. `showInHiddenAreas` decides whether a zone survives its anchor line being folded; otherwise `_computeWhitespaceProps` returns `heightInPx: 0` and `isInHiddenArea: true` and the render loop skips it — the zone is *kept*, not destroyed, so unfolding restores it instantly. `marginDomNode` is a parallel node laid out in the gutter at the same top/height, which is how a peek view draws its frame across the line-number column. `minWidthInPx` raises the editor's scroll width so a wide zone gets a horizontal scrollbar. All mutations go through a short-lived `IViewZoneChangeAccessor` that is deliberately poisoned (`invalidFunc`) after the callback returns, so nobody can stash it and mutate layout outside a batch.

**Why it matters here.** These are the bugs the deleted block API actually had — non-deterministic ordering when two plugins anchored to the same line, undefined behaviour on a folded anchor, no way to put a banner above line 1, no scroll-width contribution, no gutter twin. Nothing here is actionable now; it is a checklist for whatever replaces it, and adopting the field set up front is cheaper than the API churn afterwards.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/editorBrowser.ts:36-101 (IViewZone: afterLineNumber with 0 = before first line, afterColumn + afterColumnAffinity, showInHiddenAreas, ordinal, suppressMouseDown, heightInLines vs heightInPx, minWidthInPx, marginDomNode, onDomNodeTop, onComputedHeight), :104-125 (IViewZoneChangeAccessor addZone/removeZone/layoutZone); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/viewParts/viewZones/viewZones.ts:143-146 (_getZoneOrdinal = ordinal ?? afterColumn ?? 10000), :148-195 (_computeWhitespaceProps: validates the position, converts model→view position, and zeroes the height when the anchor line is inside a fold unless showInHiddenAreas), :198-230 (changeViewZones batches through an accessor that is invalidated after the callback), :410-412 (marginDomNode width tracks contentLeft)

**Our code.** None — the zone API this compared against is deleted. For the record, it had a row/range anchor, `top`/`bottom` surfaces with `FixedSize|MinSize|MaxSize|BoundedSize` (richer than Monaco's heightInPx/heightInLines), `left`/`right` lane surfaces with no Monaco equivalent, and a `mount` callback; it lacked `ordinal`, a 'before line 0' anchor, `showInHiddenAreas`, a margin twin, `minWidthInPx` and `suppressMouseDown`, and mutated by whole-array re-sync rather than through an add/remove/layout accessor.

**Verifier.** Scope down hard. Of the six, only an `ordinal` tiebreak (trivial — copy `InjectedTextRow.order`) and an incremental add/remove/layout accessor are worth carrying into a replacement; whole-array re-sync on every measurement tick was a real cost in the deleted design. `suppressMouseDown` is moot. The left/right lane concept had no Monaco equivalent and is worth reviving if the replacement wants it.

**How to implement.** Nothing to implement against — this is a field list for the replacement API: `ordinal?: number` as the sort key before any id fallback so ordering is deterministic regardless of provider registration order; `showInHiddenAreas?: boolean`, with the anchor resolved through `foldMap` and a `heightPx: 0` emit (keeping the widget registered) when the anchor is hidden; `minWidthPx?: number` feeding the virtualizer's `contentWidth` computation in `virtualizedTextViewGeometry`; a margin/gutter twin mount; and 'before first line' encoded explicitly in the anchor type. The accessor-batching design is the largest piece and can wait.

### Overlay widgets: declarative corner anchoring with stacking, and widget min-width feeding the editor's scroll width

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **M** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Overlay widgets are viewport-fixed rather than document-anchored. Their position is declared as one of a few named corners (or explicit coordinates), and the editor resolves it against live layout info — subtracting `2 * verticalScrollbarWidth + minimapWidth` so a find widget never sits under the minimap. `stackOrdinal` lets several widgets claim the same corner: they are sorted by ordinal and `stackCoordinates` accumulates each one's measured width (horizontal corners) or height (TOP_CENTER) so they tile instead of overlapping. `getMinContentWidthInPx()` is aggregated into `viewLayout.setOverlayWidgetsMinWidth`, so a wide overlay widget actually increases the editor's scroll width rather than being clipped. `fixedOverflowWidgets` re-bases to page coordinates with `position: fixed` for widgets that must escape a clipped container. Even the placeholder text is just an overlay widget with `position: null`, sized from `layoutInfo`.

**Why it matters here.** Our find widget, and any future 'N problems' bar, breadcrumb, or read-only banner, each hand-position themselves against the scroll element and none of them know about each other. Two of them in the top-right corner will overlap. And none of them affect scroll width, so a wide overlay is silently clipped.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/editorBrowser.ts:238-320 (OverlayWidgetPositionPreference TOP_RIGHT_CORNER/BOTTOM_RIGHT_CORNER/TOP_CENTER, IOverlayWidgetPositionCoordinates, IOverlayWidgetPosition with stackOrdinal, IOverlayWidget.getMinContentWidthInPx); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/viewParts/overlayWidgets/overlayWidgets.ts:153-166 (_updateMaxMinWidth → viewLayout.setOverlayWidgetsMinWidth), :167-213 (_renderWidget: corner placement offset by the vertical scrollbar + minimap width, stackCoordinates accumulating widths/heights for stacked widgets, fixedOverflowWidgets switching to position:fixed with page coordinates), :219-232 (render sorts by stack ordinal before laying out); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/contrib/placeholderText/browser/placeholderTextContribution.ts:64-90 (placeholder is an overlay widget with position:null, sized from layoutInfo.contentLeft/contentWidth-verticalScrollbarWidth)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/plugins.ts:208` defines `EditorOverlaySide = 'left' | 'right'` and `:227` `reserveOverlayWidth(side, width)`, whose entire implementation is `packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextView.ts:614-620`: it sets `scrollElement.style.paddingLeft/paddingRight`. That reserves *gutter* space, not a widget layer — there is no corner anchoring, no stacking, no min-content-width contribution, and no managed layer. `packages/find/src/findWidget.ts:123` creates a `.editor-find-widget` root and positions it itself; `packages/find/src/plugin.ts:138-145` owns the singleton. `packages/editor/src/style.css` has only four `z-index` declarations (2, 3, 5, 10) with no documented layering contract for plugin-supplied overlays.

**Verifier.** Keep for the concrete bug, not the abstraction. Minimum viable fix: expose the reserved left/right widths on the view-contribution context so the find widget can offset by them, exactly as Monaco subtracts `minimapWidth + 2*verticalScrollbarWidth`. A documented z-index scale for plugin overlays is the second half and is nearly free. Full declarative corner anchoring + stackOrdinal is over-engineering for a repo with one find widget.

**How to implement.** Build this as the sibling of the content-widget layer from finding #3, sharing its render tick: `packages/editor/src/editor/overlayWidgets.ts` owning one `position:absolute` layer sized to the editor box. Expose `addOverlayWidget(widget)` on `EditorViewContributionContext` with `getPosition(): {preference: 'top-right' | 'bottom-right' | 'top-center' | {top,left} | null, stackOrdinal?: number}` and optional `getMinContentWidthInPx()`. Corner offsets must subtract the minimap width — `packages/minimap` already reports its width to reserve gutter space, so route that through the same layout info. Port the `stackCoordinates` accumulation verbatim (overlayWidgets.ts:167-213); it is ~40 lines. Feed the aggregated min-width into the virtualizer's content-width computation (`virtualizedTextViewGeometry.ts`) so wide overlays extend the scroll range. Migrate `packages/find/src/findWidget.ts` as the first consumer and codify the z-index bands in `style.css` while doing it.

### Randomized differential testing of the range store against a brute-force oracle

`present` · value **medium** · effort **M** · testing · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Two complementary layers. The randomized layer drives insert/delete/change/search operations against both the real interval tree and a dumb sorted-array oracle, asserting after *every* operation that (a) the red-black invariants still hold, (b) the full in-order listing matches the oracle exactly, and (c) interval searches match. When a random run fails, `console.log(\`testIntervalTree(${JSON.stringify(this._ops)})\`)` emits the exact minimal-effort reproduction as source code, and the file shows ~25 such shrunk cases pasted in as permanent regressions. The deterministic layer, `assertNodeAcceptEdit`, is a hand-written truth table covering every stickiness value crossed with every edit-vs-range topology (edit before / at start / inside / at end / after / encompassing) and both `forceMoveMarkers` values — the cases no random generator will reliably hit.

**Why it matters here.** Range tracking through edits is exactly the kind of code where the bugs are rare topologies (an edit whose deletion exactly equals the range, an insertion at a boundary with mixed stickiness, a surrogate-pair boundary) that only surface in production as decorations drifting one character. The oracle pattern is how you get confidence cheaply, and the printed-repro trick turns every random failure into a permanent checked-in test at zero cost.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/test/common/model/intervalTree.test.ts:25-72 (Interval + Oracle: an array kept sorted, with O(n) search), :75-140 (TestState.acceptOp applies each op to both tree and oracle, then asserts assertTreeInvariants + deepStrictEqual of getAllInOrder vs the oracle's array), :173-270 (testIntervalTree(ops) replays a recorded op list; the generator prints a ready-to-paste `testIntervalTree([...])` call on failure — line 268), :275-460 (dozens of such regressions checked in verbatim), :564-700 (assertNodeAcceptEdit: an exhaustive per-stickiness table of (nodeStart, nodeEnd, stickiness, editStart, editEnd, textLength, forceMoveMarkers) → expected (start, end))

**Our code.** We have solid unit tests around the pieces (`packages/editor/src/virtualization/virtualizedTextViewModel.test.ts`, `packages/editor/src/editor/indentation.test.ts`, `packages/editor/test/inlineReplacementCaptures.test.ts`, `packages/editor/test/inlineReplacementPainting.test.ts`, plus `packages/editor/bench/pieceTable-anchors.ts` which already generates random anchors and resolves them at :136,:179), but nothing that differentially tests anchor/range survival against a brute-force model across a random edit stream. `inlineMap.ts`'s subtle rules — `anchorAfter(start)/anchorBefore(end)` (:191), the `endOffset <= startOffset` drop (:186), the overlap-suppression in `normalizeInlineRanges` (:214-221), and `resolveInlineRange` dropping deleted anchors (:230-233) — are exactly the surface an oracle test would pin down.

**Verifier.** Two genuine follow-ups survive. (1) ORACLE WEAKNESS worth fixing: `resolveAnchor` (pieceTable/anchors.ts:235-246) FALLS BACK to `resolveAnchorLinear` when the reverse index has no entry, and both paths share `resolveAnchorAgainstEntry` — so any anchor missing the index makes the assertion vacuously true. Assert index-hit coverage, or make the oracle recompute offsets from a materialized string independently. (2) The inlineMap/foldMap layer is unfuzzed: packages/editor/test/inlineMap.test.ts is 248 lines of hand-written cases (28 `it` blocks, none an edit stream), so inlineMap.ts:186 (empty-range drop), :207-221 (single-row filter + overlap suppression) and :230-233 (deleted-anchor drop) are pinned only by examples. Reusing the existing `createRandom` harness there is cheap.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/test/trackedRanges.oracle.test.ts` using bun:test. The oracle is a plain array of `{start, end, stickiness}` updated by a ~30-line reference implementation of `nodeAcceptEdit` (port Monaco's directly from intervalTree.ts:416-470 — it is the specification, not an optimization). Drive random insert/delete/replace edits through `applyBatchToPieceTable` alongside anchor-backed tracked ranges, and after each edit assert the resolved offsets match the oracle. On mismatch, `console.log` a pasteable `testTrackedRanges([...])` call exactly as Monaco does, and check the shrunk cases back in. Separately add the deterministic `assertAcceptEdit` truth table — for our two-bias anchor model that is 4 bias pairs x ~8 topologies, roughly 32 assertions, and it directly documents the semantics that `inlineMap.ts:155-158` currently only describes in a comment. This test suite is the prerequisite for landing finding #1 safely.

### Replacing a text range with an arbitrary DOM node, and collapsed marks spanning multiple lines

`missing` · value **medium** · effort **L** · feature · from **codemirror5**

**What it is.** CodeMirror's `markText` with `replacedWith: domNode` swaps a text span for a real DOM node in the inline flow. The mark is implicitly `collapsed` (the underlying text produces no glyphs) and implicitly `atomic` (`mark_text.js:205-207`), so selection and cursor motion treat it as a single indivisible unit and `reCheckSelection` pushes the caret out if an edit would strand it inside. Because collapsing is a line-*spans* operation rather than a per-line substitution, a collapsed mark may swallow whole intermediate lines: those lines get `updateLineHeight(line, 0)` and the visual line is stitched from the surrounding fragments. `handleMouseEvents: false` stamps `cm-ignore-events` so the editor's own mouse handling ignores the widget's subtree; `insertLeft` controls which side of the mark's position an insertion lands on.

**Why it matters here.** Our markdown WYSIWYG is explicitly 'a view over the same piece-table buffer'. Text-to-text substitution gets us `**bold**` → bold and `- ` → `•`, but it cannot render an inline image, a rendered LaTeX span, a clickable checkbox, or a colour swatch — all of which need a DOM node, not a string. The multi-line restriction also means a fenced code block or a multi-line link reference can't be collapsed into a single rendered widget.

**Reference.** references/codemirror5/src/model/mark_text.js:166-172 (replacedWith → marker.collapsed = true, marker.widgetNode = eltP('span',[node],'CodeMirror-widget'), cm-ignore-events unless handleMouseEvents, insertLeft), :173-179 (collapsed markers may span line boundaries; partial overlap of two collapsed markers throws), :185-198 (updateLineHeight(line,0) for fully-swallowed lines; clearOnEnter → beforeCursorEnter clears the mark), :205-217 (collapsed ⇒ atomic ⇒ reCheckSelection so the caret cannot land inside), :254-256 (widgetNode cloned per document for shared/linked docs)

**Our code.** `packages/editor/src/inlineMap.ts:207-213` (`normalizeInlineRanges`) filters `.filter(range => range.startPoint.row === range.endPoint.row)` and `.filter(range => !range.text.includes('\n'))` — replacements are strictly single-line, single-row, text-only. `InlineReplacement` (`packages/editor/src/displayTransforms.ts:173-180`) has `text: string` and no node/element field. `InlineRowSegment` (`displayTransforms.ts:184-190`) is purely column arithmetic. On the plus side, `revealInlineMap` (`inlineMap.ts:88-118`) plus `groupId` already give us the atomic/`clearOnEnter` behaviour — the caret entering a hidden span reveals the whole group — so the hard interaction semantics are solved; only the rendering substrate is missing.

**Verifier.** Split the two halves. Single-line replacedWith-DOM is worth it (markdown images, checkboxes, color swatches) and is in fact already half-built: `InlineReplacementSpec.render` and its geometry/rows plumbing ship today (`displayTransforms.ts:124`), with no production caller yet. MULTI-LINE collapsed marks should be dropped — our entire virtualizer assumes rows are the unit of layout and recycling (fixedRowVirtualizer + rowHeightIndex + displayTransforms' per-visible-row expansion at displayTransforms.ts:668-700); a mark that eats line breaks breaks that invariant everywhere. Folding already covers the real use case.

**How to implement.** The `render?: InlineReplacementRender` field already exists alongside `text` on `InlineReplacementSpec`, and the row painter already produces an `inline-block` span with `contenteditable=false` for a segment carrying it. What remains is a producer, plus the mount cache: key the mount by replacement id across row recycling or the widget will thrash on scroll. Advance-width measurement is the real cost — the segment's display width is no longer `text.length * charWidth`, so `virtualizedTextViewGeometry` needs a measured-width path per rendered replacement, fed by its own ResizeObserver. Multi-line collapsing is a separate, larger change to `foldMap`+`inlineMap` interaction and should be deferred; single-line DOM replacement alone unlocks images, checkboxes and swatches.
