## API design, options & perf infrastructure

### Debounces with no maximum wait starve syntax refresh under continuous typing

`partial` · value **high** · effort **S** · correctness · from **both**

**What it is.** CM5's `Delayed` is deliberately *not* a debounce. `set(ms, f)` replaces the callback but only touches the timer when there is no pending one, or when the new deadline is *earlier* than the pending one. A repeated `set(100, …)` therefore fires ~100ms after the *first* request, not 100ms after the last — the deadline can only be pulled in, never pushed out. That is what makes the highlight worker make forward progress while the user types continuously: `highlightWorker` slices for `workTime` ms, and when it runs out it calls `startWorker(cm, cm.options.workDelay)` (highlight_worker.js:44-47), which cannot be starved by incoming edits. Monaco expresses the same guarantee compositionally: `ThrottledDelayer` runs the delayer's trailing edge through a `Throttler`, so a new request arriving while a run is in flight is queued rather than cancelling it, and `Event.debounce` takes a `leading` flag so the first event in a burst always gets through.

**Why it matters here.** A fast typist sustains ~100-120ms between keystrokes. Our syntax refresh debounce is 150ms with no ceiling, so during a continuous typing run the editor never re-tokenizes at all — highlighting stays frozen on stale token positions until the user pauses, and the longer the run the worse the visible drift. The same applies to feature contributions (LSP diagnostics, occurrence highlights) on the same key.

**Reference.** references/codemirror5/src/util/misc.js:31-55 (class Delayed — `set(ms,f)` only re-arms when `!this.id || time < this.time`), used by references/codemirror5/src/display/highlight_worker.js:10-13 (startWorker) and re-armed mid-slice at :44-47; references/vscode/src/vs/base/common/async.ts:499-522 (ThrottledDelayer = Delayer ∘ Throttler), :421-486 (Delayer), :267-345 (Event.debounce with `leading` and `flushOnListenerRemove`)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/editor/workScheduler.ts:133-143 — `schedule` cancels the existing entry for the key (`this.cancel(options.key, 'replaced')`) and starts a fresh `delayMs` timer at :189, so re-scheduling always pushes the deadline out. There is no `maxDelayMs`. Consumers on that path: Editor.ts:2544-2553 schedules `editor.syntaxRefresh` with `delayMs: RAPID_INPUT_SECONDARY_WORK_DELAY_MS` (= 150, Editor.ts:136) on every rapid-input change, and Editor.ts:2554+ does the same for `editor.featureContributions`; syntaxController.ts:497-505 and :516-524 re-schedule with `syntaxRefreshDelay(change)`, :452 with 50ms, :360 with 120ms. The gap is already understood at one call site and worked around by hand rather than fixed in the primitive: packages/minimap/src/workerClient.ts:349-367 schedules the *same* flush under two keys — a quiet debounce at `MINIMAP_UPDATE_QUIET_DELAY_MS` plus a second entry with `replace: false` at `MINIMAP_UPDATE_MAX_DELAY_MS` — precisely to bound the wait. `budgetMs` (workScheduler.ts:334-340) bounds how long a task may *run*, not how long it may be deferred.

**Verifier.** Highest value-per-effort item in this batch. The user-visible symptom is concrete: with a 150ms pure debounce on `editor.syntaxRefresh`, a fast typist typing a long line never pauses 150ms, so syntax colouring does not update at all until they stop — colours freeze mid-line. Implementation is small because the primitive is already 90% there: add `maxDelayMs` to EditorScheduleWorkOptions and have `schedule` internally do what minimap does by hand — arm the normal replaceable entry under `key`, plus a `replace: false` entry under a derived key (e.g. `${key}#max`) at maxDelayMs, whichever fires first cancels the other. Then delete the hand-rolled pair at packages/minimap/src/workerClient.ts:349-367 and set `maxDelayMs` on Editor.ts:2544/2554. Note NOT to convert the whole scheduler to CM5's pull-in-only semantics: a pure `Delayed` would make every keystroke's syntax refresh fire on the FIRST keystroke's deadline, which is wrong for coalescing; maxDelayMs on top of a debounce is the right shape and is what minimap already proved out.

**How to implement.** Add `maxDelayMs?: number` to `EditorScheduleWorkOptions` (workScheduler.ts:56-70). Carry a `firstScheduledAt` on `ScheduledEditorWork` that survives replacement: in `schedule` (:133-143), before `this.cancel(key,'replaced')`, read the outgoing work's `firstScheduledAt` and reuse it. In `scheduleStart` (:182-190) compute `effectiveDelay = Math.min(delayMs, Math.max(0, firstScheduledAt + maxDelayMs - now))`. That is CM5's 'deadline may only be pulled in' rule expressed for a replace-on-key scheduler, and it makes the two-key minimap workaround (packages/minimap/src/workerClient.ts:349-367) deletable. Then set `maxDelayMs` at the starvation-sensitive sites: Editor.ts:2544/2554 (~400ms) and syntaxController.ts:497/516. Also consider a leading-edge option so the first keystroke of a burst refreshes immediately, matching `Event.debounce(…, leading)`.

### Font measurement: what to measure, how to key the cache, and when to throw it away

`partial` · value **high** · effort **M** · correctness · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Monaco measures by rendering each probe character repeated 2^8 = 256 times (charWidthReader.ts:112-128, using   for space so it isn't collapsed) into an off-screen container, then dividing `offsetWidth` by 256 — averaging away sub-pixel rounding that a single-glyph measurement bakes in. It measures far more than one width: `typicalHalfwidthCharacterWidth` ('n'), `typicalFullwidthCharacterWidth` ('ｍ'), `spaceWidth`, `maxDigitWidth` (max over '0'-'9', which is what makes line-number gutter width exact), middot and word-separator-middot widths for whitespace rendering, and a monospace probe: '|/-_ilm%' measured in regular *and* bold *and* italic, all compared against a reference width within ±0.001 (fontMeasurements.ts:196-215) — bold/italic breaking monospace is a real and common failure. `canUseHalfwidthRightwardsArrow` is a derived rendering decision (:217-225). Cache correctness is the other half: the key is `BareFontInfo.getId()` = pixelRatio + family + weight + size + featureSettings + variationSettings + lineHeight + letterSpacing (fontInfo.ts:104-107) — note pixelRatio is *in the key*, and PixelRatio fires whenever `matchMedia('(resolution: Ndppx)')` flips, which covers browser zoom and dragging the window to a different-DPI monitor (pixelRatio.ts:39-48). Readings taken before the webfont loaded produce garbage; the `isTrusted` flag plus a 5s eviction timer (fontMeasurements.ts:76-98) re-reads them and fires `onDidChange`, which routes straight into `_recomputeOptions`. And a defensive guard treats any width ≤ 2px as a failed read and floors it at 5 (fontMeasurements.ts:133-153).

**Why it matters here.** Every column-to-pixel mapping in a virtualized editor rides on character width. A measurement taken before a webfont finishes loading, or kept across a monitor/zoom change, silently misplaces the caret, selection rectangles, and horizontal scroll extent for the whole session with no error and no way for the user to recover short of a reload. `maxDigitWidth` and `isMonospace` additionally determine whether the gutter is wide enough and whether fast fixed-width positioning is even legal for the configured font.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/config/fontMeasurements.ts:167-245 (_actualReadFontInfo), :128-158 (readFontInfo + Bug 14341 guard), :72-98 (_writeToCache / _evictUntrustedReadings), :103-123 (serialize/restoreFontInfo); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/config/charWidthReader.ts:112-136 (_render repeats the char 256× ; fulfill = offsetWidth/256); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/config/fontInfo.ts:104-107 (getId cache key includes pixelRatio); references/vscode/src/vs/base/browser/pixelRatio.ts:39-48 (matchMedia(`(resolution: Xdppx)`) re-armed on every change); wired at references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/config/editorConfiguration.ts:94-100

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/virtualization/browserMetrics.ts:11-30 measures a single 16-char probe ('mmmmmmmmmmmmmmmm') with one `getBoundingClientRect()` and divides by 16 (:57-60) — better than one glyph, but 16× less averaging than 256, and 'm' is the widest ASCII glyph, so on a non-monospace fallback the derived width is systematically too large. We derive only `{rowHeight, characterWidth}` (browserMetrics.ts:1-4): no maxDigitWidth, no spaceWidth, no monospace verification, and no bold/italic probe at all. The cache key (browserMetrics.ts:104-119) is built from computed style (family/size/style/stretch/variant/weight/letterSpacing/lineHeight/textTransform/whiteSpace) and correctly excludes nothing font-related, but omits devicePixelRatio. Invalidation is entirely manual — `clearBrowserTextMetricsCache()` (:32-34) is exported and nothing subscribes to anything: grep for `document.fonts|fonts.ready|loadingdone|FontFaceSet` across packages/*/src returns zero hits, and grep for `matchMedia` returns only packages/decode/src/index.ts:191 (prefers-reduced-motion). packages/minimap reads `globalThis.devicePixelRatio` at snapshot time (workerClient.ts:664, :1480) but never subscribes to changes either.

**Verifier.** Keep, but split — the two halves have wildly different value. HIGH VALUE / LOW EFFORT: automatic invalidation. Subscribe to `document.fonts.ready` and the `loadingdone` FontFaceSet event and call `clearBrowserTextMetricsCache()` + re-measure; add `devicePixelRatio` to the cache key at browserMetrics.ts:104-119 and subscribe to `matchMedia('(resolution: Xdppx)')` change to invalidate. This fixes a real, reproducible bug we have today: mount before a webfont finishes loading, measure the fallback font, and every column position in the editor is wrong forever with no event that fixes it — plus the same on a drag-to-external-monitor DPR change. LOW VALUE: the 256-char probe and the extra metrics. 16 'm's already averages sub-pixel rounding 16 ways; the residual error is well under a pixel on a real monospace font, and maxDigitWidth/typicalFullwidth only earn their keep in a proportional-font renderer, which we are not. Do the invalidation half; skip the measurement-fidelity half.

**How to implement.** Extend `BrowserTextMetrics` (browserMetrics.ts:1-4) to `{rowHeight, characterWidth, spaceWidth, maxDigitWidth, isMonospace, devicePixelRatio}`. In `measureBrowserTextMetrics`, build one probe container holding regular/bold/italic child divs and append repeated-256× spans per requested char, mirroring charWidthReader.ts:65-136 — one appendChild and one batch of `offsetWidth` reads, still a single layout pass. Add `devicePixelRatio` to `browserTextMetricsCacheKey` (:104-119). Add a module-level invalidation source: a `DevicePixelRatioMonitor` copied from pixelRatio.ts:39-48 (re-arm `matchMedia` after each change — that detail is load-bearing) plus `document.fonts.addEventListener('loadingdone', …)` and a `document.fonts.ready.then(…)`, both calling `clearBrowserTextMetricsCache()` and notifying mounted editors to re-measure. That notification is the natural first consumer of the option-registry recompute from finding 1: metrics become an env input, and `lineHeight`/wrap columns fall out as computed options. Finally, port the ≤2px sanity floor (fontMeasurements.ts:133-153) and use `maxDigitWidth` for gutter sizing in packages/gutters.

### Theme scope matching should be a trie with inheritance, not exact-match plus first-segment fallback

`partial` · value **high** · effort **M** · correctness · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Theme rules are inserted into a trie keyed on dot-separated scope segments. Each node clones its parent's resolved rule when created (`new ThemeTrieElement(this._mainRule.clone())` at :404) and then `acceptOverwrite`s only the attributes the rule actually specifies — so a rule on `keyword` supplies the colour and a rule on `keyword.control` overrides only what it names, inheriting the rest. Lookup walks the trie segment by segment and, the moment a segment has no child, returns the deepest rule reached (:363-383). That means `keyword.control.conditional` resolves against `keyword.control` if that exists, else `keyword`, else the theme default — correct longest-prefix semantics at arbitrary depth. Rules are sorted lexicographically with the source index as tiebreak *before* insertion (:105-111), so a theme's declaration order can't produce specificity surprises. Results are memoized per token string in `TokenTheme._cache` (:226-244), and the resolved colours are interned through a `ColorMap` into small integer ids, which is what makes packing style into token metadata bits possible.

**Why it matters here.** Our capture→style resolution is a two-level lookup, so anything deeper than two segments loses all intermediate specificity. `keyword.control.conditional`, `keyword.control.import.from`, `variable.parameter.function`, `punctuation.definition.string.begin` all fall through to their first segment, discarding a level of styling the grammar deliberately provided. That also caps how faithfully we can render an imported VS Code/TextMate theme, and every new distinction someone wants requires editing a hardcoded table in our source rather than shipping theme data.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/languages/supports/tokenization.ts:339-410 (ThemeTrieElement.match / .insert — split on the first '.', recurse on the tail, unknown head falls back to `_mainRule`), :103-150 (resolveParsedTokenThemeRules — lexicographic sort then index, so specificity is insertion-independent; '' rules become the defaults), :193-245 (TokenTheme.match with a per-token memo cache), :277-337 (ThemeTrieElementRule.acceptOverwrite — children clone the parent rule so unspecified attributes inherit)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/syntax/captures.ts:32-53 `EXACT_CAPTURE_STYLES` (21 hardcoded full scope names) and :55-70 `PREFIX_CAPTURE_STYLES` (14 first-segment names), resolved at :76-82: exact hit, else `captureName.split('.')[0]` (:80) — nothing in between. So `keyword.control` is styled (exact entry at :37) but `keyword.control.conditional` collapses to plain `keyword`. The theme vocabulary is a closed 17-member union (packages/editor/src/theme.ts:1-19 `EditorSyntaxThemeColor`), and importing a real theme means hand-writing scope lists into those 17 slots: packages/editor/src/shiki/theme.ts:37+ `EDITOR_SHIKI_SYNTAX_SCOPE_MAPPINGS` enumerates e.g. `['entity.other.attribute-name','meta.attribute','support.type.property-name'] → 'attribute'`. No trie, no inheritance, no memo cache — `sharedStyleForTreeSitterCapture` does two object lookups and a `split` per capture, per token.

**Verifier.** Keep, heavily rescoped — the deliverable is NOT 'build Monaco's theme trie', it is 'stop the tree-sitter capture table from being the odd one out'. Concretely: replace EXACT_CAPTURE_STYLES + PREFIX_CAPTURE_STYLES in packages/editor/src/syntax/captures.ts with a single segment-keyed trie whose nodes inherit the parent's resolved style and override only the properties they name, built once at module load from the same 35 entries. That is maybe 40 lines and gets `keyword.control.conditional`, `variable.parameter.builtin`, `punctuation.bracket.angle` etc. correct instead of collapsing them. It also removes the per-token `split('.')` at captures.ts:80 (currently once per capture per token) in favour of a walk that terminates at the first missing child. Do NOT widen `EditorSyntaxThemeColor` past 17 as part of this — the shiki path already covers 'I want a real VS Code theme rendered faithfully', and theme-extract.ts is the documented bridge for the 17-slot vocabulary. Effort: low. Value: moderate, and it is entirely self-contained in one file.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/src/syntax/themeTrie.ts` porting ThemeTrieElement (tokenization.ts:339-410) essentially verbatim, but with `EditorTokenStyle` as the rule payload and an `acceptOverwrite` that merges only the defined fields of `{color, backgroundColor, fontStyle, fontWeight, textDecoration}`. Seed the trie from the existing `EXACT_CAPTURE_STYLES` + `PREFIX_CAPTURE_STYLES` tables so current behaviour is preserved exactly for the scopes they cover while deeper scopes start inheriting correctly; the tests in packages/editor/test can pin that. Add the per-capture-string memo Map from TokenTheme.match (:226-244) — `styleForTreeSitterCapture` (captures.ts:71-74) currently clones a fresh object per call, and interning the resolved style also makes the `styleKey` dedup in virtualization/sharedTokenHighlights.ts:52-66 hit more often. Then make `EditorTheme.syntax` accept arbitrary scope→style rules alongside the existing 17 named keys (theme.ts:21), and rewrite packages/editor/src/shiki/theme.ts to feed a theme's `tokenColors` straight into the trie instead of funnelling through EDITOR_SHIKI_SYNTAX_SCOPE_MAPPINGS.

### Extensible color registry with per-theme-type defaults and derived colors

`partial` · value **high** · effort **M** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Any module registers a themable color by id with a description and defaults that vary per theme type — `{light, dark, hcDark, hcLight}` — and crucially a default may be a *reference to another color id* or a *transform* over one: `registerColor('x', { dark: transparent(editorForeground, 0.3), light: … })`. `resolveColorValue` (:256-270) dispatches on the value's shape: '#rrggbb' → literal, any other string → recursive theme lookup by id, object → `executeTransform`, which itself recurses. So a theme that defines only a handful of base colors still yields a coherent full palette, and `IfDefinedThenElse`/`OneOf` express 'derive from this if the theme set it, otherwise fall back'. Emission is uniform: every registered id becomes `--vscode-<id with dots→dashes>` written into one CSS rule on the editor container (standaloneThemeService.ts:394-402), plus contributed rules from theming participants, assembled into a single string and assigned to `styleElement.textContent` once (:419-421). `StandaloneTheme.getColor` memoizes resolved defaults per id (:105-114) and lazily builds the color map only on first access (:77-93).

**Why it matters here.** Today only the core editor can be themed, and only through 23 fixed properties. A plugin — find highlights, diff gutter, minimap overlay, merge-conflict bands, scope lines — has no way to declare a themable color, so its colors are either hardcoded or require a core `EditorTheme` change. Derived defaults are the other half: a consumer supplying just a background and foreground should get sensible selection, current-line, and find-match colors without enumerating them, and today they must supply every value or accept whatever the stylesheet hardcodes.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/platform/theme/common/colorUtils.ts:168-192 (ColorRegistry.registerColor), :216-224 (resolveDefaultColor), :46-66 (ColorTransform ops: Darken/Lighten/Transparent/Opaque/OneOf/LessProminent/IfDefinedThenElse/Mix), :256-300 (executeTransform/resolveColorValue), :34-44 (asCssVariableName / asCssVariable); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/standalone/browser/standaloneThemeService.ts:382-421 (_updateThemeOrColorMap: theming participants emit rules, all registry colors emitted as CSS vars in one rule, one style element write)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/theme.ts:21-30 `EditorTheme` is 6 fixed color properties plus `syntax`; :32-40 and :44-66 map them one-to-one onto a closed list of CSS custom properties, and `applyEditorTheme` (:74-87) writes each with `element.style.setProperty` — inline styles on the host element, so there is no place for a plugin to contribute and no cascade. `mergeEditorThemes` (:89-105) is a shallow per-known-key merge and `editorThemesEqual` (:107-121) compares only the known keys, so an unknown/extension color would be invisible to both. There is no registry, no notion of light/dark/high-contrast defaults, and no color-derivation (no darken/lighten/transparent helpers anywhere under packages/editor/src). Compare our existing token-style path, which already got this right — packages/editor/src/virtualization/sharedTokenHighlights.ts:39-90 ref-counts one Highlight + one CSS rule per distinct style per document and flushes the stylesheet once per batch (:84-90); the theme layer just never grew the same structure.

**Verifier.** Keep, but the real deliverable is much narrower than the claim implies. We do not need resolveColorValue/darken/lighten — CSS color-mix already does that better, and we should keep derivation in CSS. What we actually lack is DISCOVERABILITY and TYPED PASS-THROUGH: ~80 vars exist, none is declared anywhere in TypeScript, so (a) nobody can enumerate what a theme author may set, (b) `EditorTheme` can carry only 6+17 of them, and (c) editorThemesEqual will report two themes equal when they differ on any of the other ~57. Minimal fix: a `registerEditorColor(id, cssVar, {light, dark})` table that packages append to at import time, have applyEditorTheme/mergeEditorThemes/editorThemesEqual iterate the registry instead of two frozen arrays, and let EditorTheme carry an open `colors?: Record<string,string>` bag alongside the 6 named ones. The light/dark defaults belong in `:root` + `@media (prefers-color-scheme)` in CSS as find/src/style.css already does, NOT in the JS table. Skip high-contrast entirely — that is workbench/accessibility-settings territory we have no consumer for. Effort: low-moderate. Value: moderate.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/src/themeRegistry.ts`: `registerEditorColor(id, defaults, description)` where `defaults` is `{light, dark} | ColorValue`, `ColorValue = string | ColorId | ColorTransform`, and a `resolveColorValue` port of colorUtils.ts:256-300 (start with Darken/Lighten/Transparent/OneOf/IfDefinedThenElse — the eight-op set is not needed on day one). Keep the current 23 keys as pre-registered ids so `EditorTheme` stays source-compatible, mapping them through `asCssVariableName`-style id→`--editor-*` naming; the existing EDITOR_THEME_VARIABLES/EDITOR_SYNTAX_THEME_VARIABLES tables (theme.ts:32-66) become the seed registrations. Replace `applyEditorTheme`'s per-property `setProperty` loop (:74-87) with one generated rule containing every resolved variable, written to a shared per-document `<style>` element — reuse the ref-counted, batched-flush machinery already in sharedTokenHighlights.ts:39-90 rather than inventing a second one. Then expose `registerEditorColor` through `EditorPluginContext` (packages/editor/src/plugins.ts:440-455, alongside `registerHighlighter`/`registerSyntaxProvider`) so packages/find, diff, minimap, gutters, scope-lines register their own ids and drop their hardcoded colors.

### Typed option registry: validate → compute → per-option change diff

`missing` · value **high** · effort **L** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Every option is an object with `{id (dense int), name, defaultValue, validate(input): V, compute(env, computedSoFar, value): V, applyUpdate(old, update)}`, registered into a dense array indexed by the id enum. Three layers of state are kept separate: `_rawOptions` (what callers passed, deep-cloned and migrated), `_validatedOptions` (each raw value pushed through `validate`, which clamps/coerces — `clampedInt` at editorOptions.ts:1246-1259, `boolean()` treating the string 'false' as false at :1215-1225), and `options: ComputedEditorOptions` (each validated value pushed through `compute(env, ...)`, where env carries fontInfo, outerWidth/Height, pixelRatio, viewLineCount, lineNumbersDigitCount, accessibilitySupport). Because `computeOptions` iterates the registry in id order and passes the partially-filled result in, a later option can legally read an earlier one — that's how `layoutInfo` (EditorLayoutInfoComputer at :2627-2860) is derived purely arithmetically from fontInfo + outer dimensions with zero DOM reads. Recompute is a pure function of (validated options, env); `checkEquals` deep-compares old vs new computed values slot-by-slot and produces a `boolean[]` indexed by option id, wrapped in `ConfigurationChangedEvent` so a consumer writes `if (e.hasChanged(EditorOption.lineHeight))`. If nothing changed, no event fires at all. `applyUpdate` (editorOptions.ts:1147-1167) recursively merges nested objects and reports `didChange`, so `updateOptions({minimap:{enabled:false}})` merges instead of clobbering, and a no-op update short-circuits before validation even runs. Every environmental input is wired to `_recomputeOptions` (editorConfiguration.ts:94-100): zoom, tab-focus, container resize, font measurement change, pixel ratio, accessibility, input mode.

**Why it matters here.** One `editor.updateOptions(partial)` entry point replaces N hand-written setters, and every consumer downstream gets exact change granularity for free instead of re-deriving 'did this matter to me?'. Callers get input coercion (a string '4' for tabSize, a NaN lineHeight, an out-of-range value) instead of silent corruption. And derived state like layout/wrap columns is recomputed exactly when one of its real inputs moved, not on every render.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/config/editorOptions.ts:1087-1210 (IEditorOption / BaseEditorOption / ComputedEditorOption / SimpleEditorOption / applyUpdate), :1028-1038 (ConfigurationChangedEvent.hasChanged), :5796-5801 (editorOptionsRegistry + register()); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/browser/config/editorConfiguration.ts:287-352 (EditorOptionsUtil.validateOptions/computeOptions/checkEquals/applyUpdate), :103-114 (_recomputeOptions), :165-175 (updateOptions), :258-285 (ValidatedEditorOptions / ComputedEditorOptions dense arrays)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/editor/types.ts:76-99 — `EditorOptions` is a flat readonly plain-object type with no per-option metadata. packages/editor/src/editor/Editor.ts:325-503 consumes it once in the constructor; the only validation anywhere is `normalizeTabSize(options.tabSize)` at Editor.ts:329. There is no `updateOptions`; instead there are 21 hand-written setters (Editor.ts:503,516,545,551,560,568,577,620,667,826,871,875,889,898,908,920,941,960,971,982,1001), each of which re-derives its own 'did this change / what do I need to redo' logic. The cost has already leaked into the bindings: packages/react/src/index.ts:565-590 `useControlledOptionSync` hand-writes one `useEditorLayoutEffect` per option (`syncThemeOption`, `syncHiddenCharactersOption`, `syncEditabilityOption`, `syncKeymapOption`, `syncRangeDecorationsOption`, …), and packages/solid will need the same list again. No option carries a default, a validator, or a change signal.

**Verifier.** The claim UNDERSTATES the cost — I found the drift it predicted has already happened. /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/react/src/index.ts:565-599 `useControlledOptionSync` wires 11 per-option effects (document, theme, hiddenCharacters, editability, keymap, rangeDecorations, rowGap, scrollMode, selection, scrollPosition, plugins). /Users/shaul/Desktop/D/Editor/packages/solid/src/index.ts:220-230 wires only 6 (document, theme, hiddenCharacters, editability, rangeDecorations, selection, scrollPosition) — solid silently does NOT live-update rowGap, scrollMode, or keymap. Same editor, two bindings, different sets of reactive options, and nothing in the type system notices. That divergence is the concrete user-visible bug this claim fixes, and it is the argument to keep it. Recommend the narrow version: a descriptor array (id, name, default, validate, equals, applyTo(editor)) that both bindings iterate, rather than Monaco's full compute/ComputedEditorOptions layer — we have no derived options (Monaco needs compute for wrappingInfo/layoutInfo; our layout derivation already lives in the virtualizer). Effort: moderate-low. Value: high, it deletes the drift class permanently.

**How to implement.** New `packages/editor/src/editor/optionRegistry.ts`. Define `type EditorOptionDef<K extends keyof EditorOptions, V> = { key: K; defaultValue: V; validate(input: unknown): V; compute?(env: EditorEnvironment, get: <T>(k: T) => ..., value: V): V }` and an ordered `EDITOR_OPTION_REGISTRY` array. Keep the public `EditorOptions` type exactly as-is (it is the raw input shape) and add `ResolvedEditorOptions` = the computed record. In `Editor`, replace the 21 setters' bodies with a single `updateOptions(partial: Partial<EditorOptions>)` that deep-merges into `rawOptions`, revalidates, recomputes, diffs, and dispatches through one `applyOptionChange(changed: ReadonlySet<keyof EditorOptions>)` switch that calls the existing per-concern code paths — the setters stay as thin back-compat wrappers delegating to `updateOptions`. The env object should carry `{ textMetrics, viewportWidth, viewportHeight, devicePixelRatio, lineCount }`, which we already have on `view.getState()` (Editor.ts:2486-2503), so `lineHeight`, `rowGap`, and wrap columns become computed options rather than imperatively pushed values. Then collapse packages/react/src/index.ts:565-590 into a single effect that diffs the previous options object and calls `controller.updateOptions(diff)`.

### Operation batching: coalesce DOM reads/writes across nested and repeated edits

`partial` · value **high** · effort **L** · performance · from **both**

**What it is.** CM5 wraps every public mutating method in `methodOp`, which is a re-entrancy-aware guard: if `cm.curOp` already exists the call just runs inline, otherwise it opens an operation and closes it in a `finally`. Nested calls therefore contribute to one operation instead of each doing a full display update. The payoff is `endOperations` (operations.js:57-69), which runs five explicit phases across *all* ops in the group: R1 reads (clip scrollbars, find max line, decide `mustUpdate`), W1 writes (updateDisplayIfNeeded), R2 reads (updateHeightsInViewport, measureForScrollbars, measureChar for the widest line, prepareSelection), W2 writes (set sizer minWidth, showSelection, updateScrollbars, setDocumentHeight, reset input), then `finish` (scroll into view, fire `changes`). Every layout-forcing read is hoisted above the writes that would invalidate it, so a batch of N edits costs a bounded number of forced reflows rather than N. `operation_group.js:43-72` adds the second half: `signalLater` never dispatches a handler synchronously from inside a half-updated editor — it queues into `group.delayedCallbacks` and `fireCallbacksForOps` drains with a `do…while` that keeps going while handlers append more callbacks. Monaco reaches the same shape differently: `beginEmitViewEvents`/`endEmitViewEvents` refcount a collector so nested emitters batch, outgoing events are *merged* pairwise via `attemptToMerge` before dispatch, and `_doConsumeQueue` clones the handler list and loops while the queue refills, making handler-triggered re-entrancy safe.

**Why it matters here.** Any command or plugin that performs several edits, or an edit plus a selection change plus a decoration update, currently pays full render + DOM-selection-sync + notify per step, with reads and writes interleaved so the browser is forced to reflow between them. Batching makes multi-cursor edits, sort/dedupe line ops, snippet expansion, and refactor-style multi-edit applications cost one visual update. It also removes a whole class of bugs where a listener fired mid-update observes an editor whose view and model disagree.

**Reference.** references/codemirror5/src/display/operations.js:23-53 (startOperation/endOperation), :57-69 (endOperations R1→W1→R2→W2→finish), :71-170 (phase bodies), :173-206 (runInOp/operation/methodOp/docMethodOp); references/codemirror5/src/util/operation_group.js:5-41 (pushOperation/finishOperation group nesting), :43-72 (signalLater / fireCallbacksForOps); references/vscode/src/vs/editor/common/viewModelEventDispatcher.ts:86-108 (beginEmitViewEvents/endEmitViewEvents with _collectorCnt), :42-53 (_addOutgoingEvent with attemptToMerge), :122-152 (_emitMany / _doConsumeQueue)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/editor/Editor.ts:2388-2421 `applySessionChange` synchronously does `renderSessionChange` → `revealOffset` → `inputSelection.syncDomSelection()` → `sessionOptions.onChange` → `notifyViewContributions` → `notifyChangeWithTiming`, with no guard for an already-in-progress operation and no phase separation. `EditorEditInput = TextEdit | readonly TextEdit[]` (types.ts:118) lets a *single* call carry a batch, but two consecutive `edit()` calls, or an edit followed by `setSelection`, each run the full pipeline. Layout-forcing reads are scattered through the write path: virtualizedTextViewGeometry.ts:689,697,700,709 (`getBoundingClientRect`), inputSelectionController.ts:800,1045,1050,1057 (rect + `scrollHeight`/`clientHeight`), interleaved with writes at virtualizedTextViewRows.ts:1913,1939,1952 and Editor.ts:1145 (`scrollTop =`). Grep for `runInOp|startOperation|beginUpdate|batch` in packages/editor/src returns only packages/react/src ReactEditorStore.batch, which batches React notifications, not DOM work.

**Verifier.** Keep, but rescope to the two cheap halves rather than porting CM5's five-phase endOperations. (1) An operation depth counter around the public mutating methods so `edit(); setSelection()` produces ONE reveal + ONE syncDomSelection + ONE onChange instead of two — reuse the exact `notifying`/`pendingLayout` pattern already proven in viewContributions.ts:51-83, hoisted to Editor. (2) A per-operation rect cache keyed on the row element, cleared at operation close — that alone kills the repeated `row.element.getBoundingClientRect()` in measuredRangeRect/measuredElementRect, which is the worst offender because it re-measures the SAME row rect once per range on a row. The full R1/W1/R2/W2/finish phase split is not worth it: our writes are already funnelled through the virtualizer rather than scattered across independent addons the way CM5's are.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/src/editor/operation.ts` with an `EditorOperation` accumulator on `Editor` (`private currentOp: EditorOperation | null`) and `runInOperation<T>(fn: () => T): T` using the exact CM5 re-entrancy guard (`if (this.currentOp) return fn()`). Wrap the public mutators (`edit`, `setText`, `setSelection`, `setRangeDecorations`, `setRowDecorations`, `setSyntaxFolds`, and `commandRouter.execute`) in it. Move the body of `applySessionChange` (Editor.ts:2388-2421) into the operation flush, split as: R phase — read `view.getState()`, current scroll metrics, and any `getBoundingClientRect` the selection layer needs (inputSelectionController.ts:800,1045); W phase — `renderSessionChange`, scroll writes, `showSelection`; finish phase — reveal, then drain the queued notifications. Add a `signalLater`-equivalent: instead of calling `sessionOptions.onChange` / `notifyViewContributions` / `notifyChangeWithTiming` inline, push them onto `currentOp.deferredCallbacks` and drain with the `do…while` refill loop from operation_group.js:19-29, merging consecutive same-kind view-contribution notifications the way `_addOutgoingEvent` does. Existing `appendTiming` calls map cleanly onto the phases.

### DisposableStore and a pluggable disposable tracker for leak detection

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **S** · testing · from **monaco**

**What it is.** `IDisposable` is a one-method interface, exactly like ours, but every construction and disposal routes through two global hooks — `trackDisposable(x)` and `markAsDisposed(x)` — that are no-ops unless a tracker is installed. `DisposableStore.add` also calls `setParentOfDisposable(child, this)`, so the tracker learns the ownership tree, not just a flat set. In tests a tracker is installed that captures a stack per live disposable; `computeLeakingDisposables` walks the graph and reports the ones that were constructed, parented, and never disposed, with the *construction* stack attached and boilerplate frames stripped (:178). `GCBasedDisposableTracker` (:49-83) is the zero-overhead production variant: it registers each disposable with a `FinalizationRegistry` and reports only those collected without ever having been disposed. The ergonomic layer on top — `toDisposable(fn)`, `MutableDisposable` (swap-and-dispose-previous in one assignment), `DisposableMap`, and the `dispose()` overloads that accept a single value, an array, or any iterable — is what makes the pattern cheap enough that people actually use it consistently.

**Why it matters here.** We have a large plugin surface where every contribution point hands back an `EditorDisposable`, and correctness depends on every host and every plugin disposing every one on unmount. Right now that is enforced only by hand-maintained arrays and code review; a plugin that forgets one leaves a listener, a decoration, or a scheduler entry alive across editor remounts with no test that can catch it.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/base/common/lifecycle.ts:229-233 (setDisposableTracker), :85-180 (DisposableTracker — records a Stacktrace per disposable, computeLeakingDisposables, removePrefix noise-trimming of the captured frames), :49-83 (GCBasedDisposableTracker using FinalizationRegistry), :271-284 (trackDisposable/markAsDisposed/setParentOfDisposable), :327-360 (dispose() overloads over single/array/iterable), :380-400 (Disposable base class registering itself with the tracker)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/plugins.ts:27-29 `EditorDisposable`, with `disposeAll(registrations)` and manual arrays repeated at :791, :796, :855, :873, :1036, :1062, :1088, :1112, :1138, :1164, :1300 and a `disposeAll(result)` aggregator at :1311 — the same store-shaped logic re-implemented per contribution kind. `trackLifecycleRegistration` (plugins.ts, used at :1029 and :1055) and `createEditorLifecycleSummary` (Editor.ts:263) show we already want lifecycle visibility, but there is no construction-site attribution and nothing that can answer 'which disposables from this mount are still alive'. There is no `MutableDisposable` equivalent — swap-and-dispose is hand-written, e.g. plugins.ts:1226 and :1274 (`this.…InvalidationDisposables.get(provider)?.dispose()` before re-registering). packages/editor/src/editor/pluginLifecycle.test.ts exists, so there is already a place for the assertion to live.

**Verifier.** Keep — but for a hole I found while refuting it, not for the tracker. plugins.ts:1279-1284 reads `if (registrations) registrations.push(disposable)` and SILENTLY DROPS the disposable when `currentLifecycleRegistrations()` returns null (:1286-1288). That is null for every registration made outside the synchronous install/activate body — i.e. anything a plugin registers from an async continuation, a timer, or an event handler after activation. Those registrations are never auto-disposed on unmount and never appear in the lifecycle summary (Editor.ts:263 createEditorLifecycleSummary / :2665). That is a real leak class in a plugin system where async registration is normal (LSP capabilities arriving after initialize, for instance), and it is invisible today. Minimal fix: have the plugin context carry its own owning store so late registrations attribute to the plugin rather than to nothing, and make the null branch loud (log or throw in dev) instead of silent. Add MutableDisposable while there — it is ~10 lines and deletes the hand-written swap at plugins.ts:1226/:1274. SKIP the global tracker + stack capture: with implicit per-lifecycle attribution already in place, the leak surface it would find is almost entirely the null branch above, which is cheaper to close directly. Assertion belongs in the existing packages/editor/src/editor/pluginLifecycle.test.ts.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/src/editor/disposables.ts` with `EditorDisposableStore` (add/clear/dispose, disposes immediately if the store is already disposed — lifecycle.ts:410-460), `toEditorDisposable(fn)`, and `MutableEditorDisposable`. Route creation and disposal through two module-level hooks `trackDisposable`/`markAsDisposed` with a `setEditorDisposableTracker(tracker | null)` setter, defaulting to null so production cost is zero (lifecycle.ts:229-284). Replace the eleven ad-hoc arrays in plugins.ts with stores and the two manual swap sites (:1226, :1274) with `MutableEditorDisposable`. Then in packages/editor/test add a helper that installs a stack-capturing tracker around a mount/unmount cycle and asserts nothing survives — wire it into pluginLifecycle.test.ts first, then make it a shared fixture so every plugin package (find, minimap, gutters, diff, lsp-plugin) gets the same assertion for free.

### Emitter/Event: lazy wiring, listener-error isolation, re-entrancy-safe delivery, leak detection

`partial` · value **medium** · effort **M** · api-design · from **monaco**

**What it is.** Four things a bare callback field doesn't give you. (1) Lazy wiring: `onWillAddFirstListener`/`onDidRemoveLastListener` let an emitter attach its own upstream subscription only while someone is listening — that's how `Event.debounce` (:267-345) sets up and tears down its timer and how DOM listeners get attached on demand. (2) Error isolation: `_deliver` (:1376-1395) wraps each listener in try/catch routed to `onListenerError`, so one throwing subscriber cannot prevent the remaining subscribers from seeing the event. (3) Re-entrancy: `fire` with multiple listeners enqueues into a shared `EventDeliveryQueuePrivate` holding `{current emitter, value, i, end}` and `_deliverQueue` increments `dq.i` *before* invoking the listener (:1398-1404) — if a listener re-enters `fire()`, the outer dispatch is drained to completion first (:1411-1413) rather than interleaving. (4) Leak detection: past a threshold the emitter records a stack per listener, reports the most frequent one as 'dominated' or 'popular' (:1264-1277), and past threshold² refuses new listeners outright. On top of this, `Event` is a set of composable operators — debounce/throttle/accumulate/once/filter/map/runAndSubscribe — that any producer gets for free because the producer is just a function `(listener) => IDisposable`.

**Why it matters here.** We already have the *disposable* half of this contract everywhere; what's missing is a producer primitive. Concretely: today one throwing `onChange` handler or one throwing view contribution can abort the rest of the notification pass mid-edit; there is no way to hand a consumer a debounced or throttled view of an editor event without them writing their own timer; and a plugin that leaks subscriptions across mount/unmount is invisible until memory grows.

**Reference.** references/vscode/src/vs/base/common/event.ts:1170-1230 (Emitter construction + options), :1260-1350 (get event — leak monitor, listener containers, onWillAddFirstListener/onDidAddFirstListener/onDidRemoveLastListener), :1376-1435 (_deliver with per-listener try/catch, _deliverQueue, fire), :1441-1480 (EventDeliveryQueuePrivate — index-based cursor so re-entrant fire() finishes the outer dispatch first), :1002+ (LeakageMonitor with most-frequent-stack attribution and hard refusal past threshold²), :267-345 (Event.debounce), :347-368 (Event.accumulate), :373-430 (Event.throttle)

**Our code.** packages/editor/src/plugins.ts:27-29 defines `EditorDisposable {dispose(): void}` and it is used consistently as the *subscription* return type (:106, :417, :447-453), but there is no `Emitter`/`Event<T>` producer type anywhere — grep for `class .*Emitter` under packages/*/src returns nothing. Producers are ad-hoc optional callbacks: `options.onChange` invoked bare at Editor.ts:2515 and :2522, `sessionOptions.onChange?.(finalChange)` at Editor.ts:2412, `provider.onDidChangeBlocks?.(…)` at plugins.ts:1207, `onDidChangeInjectedTextRows?.(…)` at :1255, `EditorTextBufferChangeListener` at documentSession.ts:110, `ConflictListener` at mergeConflictPlugin.ts:56. Error handling is per-site and inconsistent — plugins.ts:1033-1038 wraps a registration notification and rolls back on throw, but the change-notification path (Editor.ts:2412-2419) has no try/catch around individual consumers. No debounce/throttle/accumulate operators exist over these callbacks; each consumer that wants one reaches for `EditorWorkScheduler` directly.

**Verifier.** Keep, but buy only the parts that pay. WORTH IT: (1) error isolation — a `notifyAll(listeners, arg)` helper wrapping each call in try/catch and routing throws to the existing logger (packages/editor/src/logging) instead of aborting the fan-out; that is ~15 lines and fixes a real silent-data-loss path at documentSession.ts:645. (2) iterate a snapshot copy so subscribe/unsubscribe during dispatch is well-defined. (3) collapse the three hand-rolled Sets onto one tiny `EditorEventSource<T>` returning EditorDisposable, so the subscription+dispose contract is written once. NOT WORTH IT: Monaco's onWillAddFirstListener/onDidRemoveLastListener lazy wiring (our producers are already cheap fields on objects that exist anyway — there is no expensive upstream to attach), and Event.debounce/throttle/accumulate operators (we already have a far more capable scheduler in workScheduler.ts with task classes, budgets and abort; adding a second, weaker deferral mechanism would be a step back — consumers reaching for EditorWorkScheduler is the right answer, not a workaround). Effort: low for the kept part. Value: moderate.

**How to implement.** Add `packages/editor/src/editor/emitter.ts`: `type EditorEvent<T> = (listener: (e: T) => void, disposables?: EditorDisposable[]) => EditorDisposable` and `class EditorEmitter<T> { readonly event: EditorEvent<T>; fire(e: T): void; dispose(): void }`. Port three pieces from event.ts and skip the rest: per-listener try/catch routed to an injectable `onListenerError` (:1376-1395), the index-cursor delivery queue for re-entrancy (:1441-1470 — the array-snapshot-plus-index shape, not the shared cross-emitter queue), and `onWillAddFirstListener`/`onDidRemoveLastListener` hooks. Add `EditorEvent.debounce/throttle/once/map` as free functions over the type, implemented on top of our existing `EditorWorkScheduler` rather than raw timers so debounced events inherit priority, cancellation, and the `maxDelayMs` fix from finding 4. Migrate incrementally: `Editor.onChange` and the plugin-host `onDidChange*` hooks first (Editor.ts:2513-2523, plugins.ts:1207/1255), keeping the existing option-callback signatures as thin adapters. In test builds only, wire a listener-count threshold that logs the dominant registration stack (event.ts:1264-1277), which would catch plugin subscription leaks across the mount/unmount cycles in pluginLifecycle.test.ts.
